Each unit says what it says and why it comes here, as the text itself gives the reason; a ▲ marks an order the text never justifies. Paragraph chips name what a paragraph does: claim, evidence, definition, derivation, method, code, result, caveat, transition, example, takeaway, exercise.

The book as a whole

Claim
As the preface argues it, asset allocation is hard in the chain of implementation decisions (data, estimation, solver, costs) and not in the ideas, so the book states each method, attributes it, runs it on a modeled market, and measures how much of the textbook answer survives out of sample.
What the chapters jointly argue
  • The mean cannot be estimated and the covariance can, so the rules that estimate no mean lead the out-of-sample register (chapters 4, 6, 8, 9, 25).
  • On sixteen classes almost every refinement changes little or cannot be told from sampling error: expected shortfall for variance (10), higher moments (11), signals other than the term spread (16), regimes detected in real time (19), robustness sets (28), and the nested allocation loses to the flat one (15).
  • Constraints are the precondition for a portfolio anyone would hold, and a binding constraint is a cost with a price (12, 20, 22).
  • The package accepts what it does not honor, so every solved weight vector is audited (9, 10, 12, 13, 22, 23, 25).
  • Forecast error should be priced in the investor's metric and not in squared error (26, 27).
Order
Prerequisites (1–2), Foundations of Portfolio Choice (3–5), Estimating the Inputs (6–8), Portfolio Optimization (9–14), Allocation Strategies (15–20), Inside the Sleeves (21–22), Implementation and Evaluation (23–25), Advanced Topics (26–28).
Why this order
One sentence in the preface: the parts follow the allocation hierarchy, classes in Parts II to V, securities in VI, implementation in VII, theory in VIII; strategic versus tactical is a second axis labeled chapter by chapter. Part I is never placed, no part has an introduction of its own, and no text says why the inputs (III) come before the optimizer (IV) or why evaluation (VII) comes after the strategies it scores.
Dependencies against the order
  • Chapter 1 borrows its notation and its market from chapter 2.
  • Chapter 2 runs chapter 9's long-only tangency portfolio to justify the euro-area overlay, before the reader has met an optimizer.
  • Chapters 6, 7 and 8 use chapter 9's problem, its tangency and minimum-variance equations and its frontier as if already read.
  • Chapter 8 takes its rolling scheme and the register from chapter 25; every construction chapter from 8 onward reads that register, which chapter 25 introduces.
  • Chapter 12 borrows chapter 13's maxSR verification and its audit() function.
  • Chapter 18 uses chapter 23's net return and turnover and chapter 25's Sharpe standard error and paired test, in the past tense.
  • Chapter 19 needs chapter 23's cost accounting and chapter 25's register.
  • Chapter 20 is built on chapter 21's equity sleeve and on chapter 24's tracking-error and standard-error equations.
  • Chapter 21 imports its whole scoring apparatus from chapter 24: active weights, information ratio, the fundamental law, the standard error of the ratio.
  • Chapter 23 computes none of its numbers; it reads them from the runs of chapter 25.
Patterns across chapters
  • The section called Implementation carries the results, and the section meant as the application re-reads them or adds one more case: chapters 4, 5, 10, 11, 16, 17, 18, 19, 26 and 27. In 15 the theory does the application's work in advance; in 14 each section does the next one's job.
  • The chapter's central claim sits in a late paragraph and not under a heading or in the theory: 8 (last paragraph of a subsection), 17 (application, fourth paragraph), 22 (one theory paragraph without an equation), 27 (end of the implementation).
  • Objects the later code depends on are defined inside collapsed worked boxes or callouts: 6, 10, 11.
  • Theory that is defined and then never used in the body: lower partial moments and drawdown measures (10), conditional comoments (11), C-RAFE and the nested measures (26, exercises only), robust optimization and smooth ambiguity (28).
  • Theory sections that already compute or run the package: 3, 13, 14, 15.
  • Results stated three or four times: the two numbers of chapter 13, the reporting rule in chapter 10, the answer of chapter 8, the chapter anatomy in the preface.
  • Figures drawn and never read in the prose (4, 15, 16, 19) and tables printed and never read (7, 12, 17).
  • Main section headings with no paragraph of their own: 10, 15, 18.
  • Two chapters inside one frame: 1 (toolchain and the boundary argument, interleaved), 3 (the conventions, then the investor); chapter 2 is a reference chapter with no theory, implementation and application at all.
  • Counts that do not close: chapter 9 points three times to a callout on maxSR that does not exist; "three ways" that are four in 9 and 17; a signal count in 16 that names four families and claims three.
  • Takeaways and exercises run over the contract almost everywhere (six to eight takeaways, four or five exercises), and in 11 and 21 the takeaways omit the application's finding.
  • Lengths differ by a factor of three: chapters 13, 14, 23 and 25 have about 3,300 words, chapter 9 has 10,239, most others 6,500 to 8,000.
Open at the top level
  • Evaluation is used before it is taught. Should the accounting of Part VII (net return, turnover, the register, the Sharpe standard error, the information ratio) come before the strategies that are scored with it?
  • The inputs are estimated for an optimizer the reader has not met. Should chapter 9 precede Part III, or should Part III stop using its equations?
  • Is "Implementation" the method and "Application" the result? Ten chapters put the result into the implementation.
  • Does the book want parts with a text of their own, saying what the part argues and why it sits where it does?

What readers proposed

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Chapter 10: move the risk measures themselves (the axioms, value at risk, expected shortfall, the downside and drawdown families) into the first content part, and leave the optimization on them in Part IV. The outline agrees from two sides: chapter 10 takes expected shortfall and the Cornish-Fisher expansion from chapter 4, and chapter 4 tabulates the Sharpe ratio and the maximum drawdown before either is defined.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Chapter 14: close the arc to the global minimum-variance portfolio and bring in the family of risk-based rules that the *Lost in Translation* paper runs side by side (minimum correlation, inverse volatility and the rest of its roster). Hierarchical risk parity being in the book is welcomed. In the text today: equal risk contribution and inverse volatility are in chapter 14, maximum diversification is named once, minimum correlation appears nowhere, hierarchical risk parity is a box in chapter 15, and the register scores five rules.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Level against audience: the reader is a master's student or a practitioner; chapter 8 reads as cutting-edge research (meant as praise) and chapter 11 as advanced because of the comoment tensors. The author's position in the same exchange: the brief was the level and the order of the two model books, papers included. The preface names only Part VIII as material that can be skipped.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Chapters 12 and 13 are sensible as they are.

Front matter

Says
The preface says why the book exists, whom it serves, how a chapter is built, how the parts are ordered, what the data are, and how to rebuild the book.
Why here
Not stated beyond its being the preface.
Note
The chapter anatomy is told in full twice, Part VIII is described three times, and part numbers are used one section before the parts are laid out; that layout never mentions Part I.

Preface

1463 words · 10 sections · 20 paragraphs

Claim
The difficulty of asset allocation lives in the chain of implementation decisions rather than in the ideas, so the book states each method in mathematics, attributes it, runs it in R on a fully inspectable modeled market, and measures how much of the textbook answer survives out of sample.
Question
Why can a student who derives the tangency portfolio on paper not produce it from a return matrix; posed as an observation, nobody is cited for it (the first citations are the package, @peterson2026, and the two model books, @scheuch2023 and @coqueret2020).
Why here
Needs nothing from earlier chapters; sends the reader forward to chapters 1 to 3 for setup, notation, data and the strategic/tactical vocabulary, and to the appendices for data, proofs, package notes, the R primer, solutions, colophon and acknowledgements; why it sits first is not stated beyond its being the preface.
Does
Know what the book is and whom it serves, how a chapter and the parts are shaped, what every number is computed on, what can be skipped, the notation and code conventions, and how to rebuild the book.
Order
Why the book exists, who it is for, how a chapter is built, how the parts are organized, the data, what to skip, what the book is not, conventions, reproduction, acknowledgements; the chapter contract (theory, implementation, application, takeaways, exercises) does not apply to front matter and none of its elements appear.

Why this book exists 324 words

Says
Asset allocation is easy to derive and hard to produce, the package that does the work is underused and mis-defaulted, and this book is the course text that closes that gap.
Why here
not stated; the page description lists the first three sections in this order and gives no reason.
  1. claim ¶1 Allocation is taught as a few ideas and practiced as a chain of decisions; the gap is the difficulty.
  2. claim ¶2 The package is broad, underused and wrongly defaulted; the book is a course text built on it.
  3. method ¶3 The models are Tidy Finance and Coqueret–Guida; this book begins where their factors and forecasts end.

Note ¶2 does two things: the package's underuse and wrong defaults, then the book's own program (mathematics, attribution, R, out-of-sample test); that program is the preface's thesis and sits mid-paragraph.

Who this is for 224 words

Says
Written first for the master's student, in the same text for the practitioner, and usable by the researcher as a route into the literature.
Why here
takes "course text" from the previous section's ¶2 and names the readers; no reason for the order is given.
  1. claim ¶1 Written first for a master's student; one chapter per session, its anatomy sketched, prerequisites named.
  2. claim ¶2 Also for the practitioner; one track serves both, and the callouts carry what the package makes hard.
  3. claim ¶3 Not aimed at researchers, but the references are complete enough to serve as a route into the literature.

Note ¶1 already gives the chapter anatomy (question and its source, theory, runnable code, takeaways, exercises, solutions appendix) that the next section repeats.

How the chapters are built 158 words

Says
Every chapter runs question, what we do, theory, implementation, application, takeaways and exercises, with the mathematics in the text, proofs sent out except in Part VIII, and package traps in callouts.
Why here
expands the chapter sketch given in the previous section's ¶1; not stated why it comes before the part structure.
  1. method ¶1 Every chapter: question and its source, what we do, theory, implementation, application, takeaways, exercises, solutions in appendix.
  2. method ¶2 Mathematics in the running text; Parts II–IV send proofs to the appendix, Part VIII derives in place.
  3. method ¶3 Package traps go in callouts where the reader would meet them; skippable worked examples in collapsible boxes.

Note ¶1 restates the previous section's ¶1 almost item by item, down to the same pointer to the solutions appendix; ¶2 uses part numbers before the next section says what the parts are.

How the book is organized 136 words

Says
The parts follow the allocation hierarchy from classes down to securities, and a second, orthogonal distinction between strategic and tactical allocation is labeled chapter by chapter.
Why here
not stated; the previous section already refers to Parts II–IV and VIII, which this section is the first to lay out.
  1. claim ¶1 Parts follow the allocation hierarchy: classes in II–V, securities in VI, implementation in VII, theory in VIII.
  2. definition ¶2 Strategic versus tactical runs across the parts; defined in chapter 3, then labeled chapter by chapter.

Note ¶1 places Parts II through VIII and never mentions Part I, which the "What you can skip" section later counts as part of the course.

The data 112 words

Says
Every number is computed on a modeled multi-asset market shipped with the companion package, which is what makes the book reproduce offline; real data are used only where a claim is about real markets.
Why here
not stated; the first section's ¶2 promised "a market whose every property can be inspected" and this section names it.
  1. definition ¶1 The modeled market: its size and span, three pointers, why modeled not recorded, and when real data are used.

Note one paragraph carries four things: the inventory, the three forward pointers, the modeled-not-recorded claim and its payoff, and the real-data exception.

What you can skip 48 words

Says
Parts I to VII are the complete course; Part VIII is optional theory that nothing earlier depends on.
Why here
not stated; it needs the part layout of "How the book is organized" and could sit directly under it, but the data section stands between them.
  1. claim ¶1 Parts I–VII are the course and complete alone; Part VIII is theory nothing earlier depends on.

Note Part VIII is described for the third time (the chapter-anatomy section ¶2, the organization section ¶1, and here), each time in slightly different words.

What this book is not 97 words

Says
The book is neither an R introduction nor an empirical asset-pricing text, and it delegates both, by citation, to three named books.
Why here
not stated.
  1. caveat ¶1 Not an introduction to R or to empirical asset pricing; those are cited rather than reproduced.
  2. caveat ¶2 (list) Three delegations: data and factors to Tidy Finance, prediction to Coqueret–Guida, R to Wickham and the primer.

Note the two model books return from the first section's ¶3 with the same point ("picks up where the prediction ends" restates "where those books end, this one begins"); the pointer to the R primer repeats "Who this is for" ¶1.

Conventions 203 words

Says
Notation, return basis, code naming, the per-chapter setup lines, the asserted alignments, and the typographic rules are fixed here once.
Why here
not stated.
  1. definition ¶1 Notation: bold vectors and matrices fixed in chapter 2; simple returns in excess of the bill; code names mirror symbols.
  2. method ¶2 Chapter setup: project root, one setup file, tidypm, the explicit long-to-wide crossing, alignment asserted, figures from one frame.
  3. definition ¶3 Numbers computed, never typed; American spelling in prose, package spelling in code; functions in code font with parentheses.

Note ¶2 mixes what the reader types (the setup lines, the one crossing) with the author's own coding discipline (assertions, one data frame per figure), which the reader never has to act on.

Why here
not stated.
  1. code colophon
  2. method ¶1 renv.lock pins the dependencies; deviations listed in the package-notes appendix, the build recorded in the colophon.

IPrerequisites

Says
The tools and the data are fixed once: the crossing between the long table and the date-indexed matrix, guarded by assertions (1), and the notation, the modeled market with its calendar, and the real-data layer (2).
Why here
Stated in the chapters only: the boundary "is crossed in every chapter and is therefore fixed here once" (1), and the notation is fixed "so that no later chapter redefines a symbol" (2). The preface's account of the parts leaves Part I out.
Note
Chapter 1 interleaves two subjects, the toolchain and the boundary argument, and borrows its market from chapter 2. Chapter 2 is a reference chapter; nearly half of it is the modeled-market section, and its caveats subsection runs an optimizer from chapter 9.

1 Setting Up Your Environment

3514 words · 5 sections · 45 paragraphs

Claim
The long table and the date-indexed matrix are both unavoidable, so the crossing between them is made in one explicit call each way and guarded by assertions, because it fails in three ways that nothing downstream reports.
Question
What must be installed, and in what shape must a return series arrive, before any portfolio can be computed; cites @wickham2014 for the tidy table and @zeileis2005 for the ordered, date-indexed matrix.
Why here
First chapter, so it needs nothing earlier; the text says the boundary is crossed in every chapter and is therefore fixed here once; it borrows its notation and its market from ch. 2 ahead of time, says its rule recurs in ch. 9 and ch. 25 and its conventions are fixed in ch. 4, and places itself on ch. 3's horizon axis as allocating nothing.
Does
Install the toolchain, open a chapter with the five standard lines, move a panel between tibble and xts in one call each way, and assert that weights and columns share one index before computing anything.
Order
Opening (question, roadmap, installation, package table, callout), theory (two data models), implementation (the crossing and its three failures), application (code conventions, the data's shape, reproducibility), takeaways, exercises; deviations: five paragraphs and six chunks of installation sit in the opening before the theory, and two of the three application subsections are the book's conventions and pins rather than an application, so no picture opens it.

Opening (before the first heading) 897 words

Says
What to install, and that the two canonical shapes of a return series disagree, so the boundary between them is fixed here once.
Why here
The chapter's own roadmap puts installation first, then the data models; no other reason given.
  1. code setup
  2. claim ¶1 Poses two questions, installation and data shape; the second has two canonical answers that disagree, fixed here once.
  3. method ¶2 Roadmap: install, the two models, cross and break the boundary three ways, read the data once; allocates nothing.
  4. method ¶3 Three programs: R from 4.4, an editor, Quarto only to render; the CRAN packages install in one call.
  5. code install-cran
  6. method ¶4 tidypm is off CRAN: the modeled market, the two coercion functions, what the package lacks; installs from GitHub.
  7. code install-tidypm
  8. method ¶5 renv.lock pins every version; renv restores it so printed numbers reproduce to the last digit.
  9. code restore
  10. code packages
  11. code tbl-01-packages
  12. evidence ¶6 Attributes each table row to its citation; solvers are backends picked by name, ch. 13 and app. C say which.
  13. claim callout ¶7 PortfolioAnalytics exports more names than it has functions.
  14. code namespace
  15. caveat callout ¶8 The _v1/_v2 exports are a legacy interface, why old forum answers fail; the book uses only three unsuffixed functions.
  16. code namespace-data
  17. caveat callout ¶9 The package fetches no data and bundles almost none; it optimizes what it is handed, which is this chapter's subject.

Note The chapter's motivation (handing the optimizer the right object) arrives last, inside the callout, after five paragraphs of installation; the callout carries its own ## heading, which a heading count would take for a sixth section.

Two Data Models 563 words

Says
A return panel has two layouts, long for building and wide for computing, neither can be dropped, and the three equations of the wide form name the three ways the crossing fails.
Why here
Second in the roadmap; the text says the equations state what can go wrong before any code does, and the implementation section produces each failure from them.
  1. definition ¶1 A return panel is numbers indexed by date and asset; two layouts, each makes some operations natural, others awkward.
  2. definition ¶2 The tidy layout: one observation per row; filter, join, grouped mutate apply directly; how sleeves, screens and figures are built.
  3. definition ¶3 The wide layout: a T×N matrix with ordered dates; defines R, r_t, portfolio return and sample moments in three equations.
  4. claim ¶4 zoo made the layout an object, xts specialized it to time; both analytics packages accept it and nothing else.
  5. claim ¶5 Neither layout can be dropped; the crossing has a fixed direction; timetk and tsibble hide it and are avoided.
  6. claim ¶6 The three equations yield three failure modes: ragged panel, weights paired by position, duplicate keys; the next section produces each.

Note ¶3 carries all three numbered equations in one paragraph, in a notation the text says ch. 2 fixes (a forward dependency); the section has no code.

The Tibble–xts Boundary 991 words

Says
tidypm crosses the boundary in one call each way, the round trip is lossless, and the chapter's assumptions about R are asserted where the matrix is built.
Why here
The roadmap's third step; needs the matrix and the three failure modes of the theory section; builds the R, rf and few objects that the subsection and the application reuse.
  1. method ¶1 as_xts_pm() takes a long table and three column names to the matrix; as_pm() takes it back; the data ship long.
  2. code long-form
  3. code data
  4. code ¶2 The assertions state what the chapter assumes about R and fail here, naming the condition, not chapters later.
  5. code wide-form
  6. code ¶3 The reverse crossing returns a three-column tibble, and the round trip changes nothing.
  7. code back-again

Note Three short paragraphs around four chunks; the subsection below carries five-sixths of the section's words.

Three ways the boundary breaks 829 words

Says
Each failure mode is manufactured from the book's data so the reader meets its message here, and the guard in every case is to index by name and assert that the names agree.
Why here
The text says the failures are produced here so their messages are printed first; the order ragged, column order, duplicate keys is the order the equations gave them; the closing rule is said to recur in ch. 9 and ch. 25.
  1. method ¶1 Each failure is manufactured from the class returns so its message is printed here before the reader meets it.
  2. method ¶2 A ragged panel: drop one row, one asset's first observation, and cross.
  3. code ragged-build
  4. result ¶3 The coercion pads and warns; the asset's column moves; mean and covariance use different samples; the optimizer accepts it silently.
  5. code ragged-optimizer
  6. result ¶4 Weights barely move here; in ch. 25's windows and ch. 21's sleeve the gap matters; handle it before crossing.
  7. caveat ¶5 Column order: the table's sort order becomes the matrix's column order, so a join or arrange() reorders the columns.
  8. code column-order
  9. method ¶6 Apply a 60/40 weight vector built in the original order to the reordered matrix; Return.portfolio() pairs by position.
  10. code column-order-cost
  11. result ¶7 The positional portfolio holds two wrong assets and nothing says so; the guard is the shared index of @eq-01-portfolio-return.
  12. code column-order-assert
  13. caveat ¶8 The same holds leaving the optimizer: extractWeights() is ordered by the fitted matrix; applied by position elsewhere, every entry mis-pairs.
  14. caveat ¶9 Duplicate keys: a repeated date–asset pair describes no matrix, and the failure surfaces in two steps.
  15. code duplicate-keys
  16. result ¶10 The warning names the cause, the error only the symptom; the check belongs in the long table, before crossing.
  17. code duplicate-check
  18. claim ¶11 The book's rule, stated here: index by name, then assert agreement, failing the render; recurs in ch. 9 and 25.

Note The governing rule (¶11) comes after all three demonstrations, not before them; ¶3 does three jobs (column move, set.portfolio.moments() trap, ch. 9 pointer); column order gets twice the space of either other case.

Every chapter opens with the same five lines, and the prose, figures and spelling follow three conventions.

Why here

Reproducibility 136 words

Says
The seed, the package versions and the dataset version are pinned, and the dataset pin matters most because a regenerated market is a different path.
Why here
not stated; it completes the renv paragraph of the opening and closes the chapter.
  1. method ¶1 Three pins, seed, lockfile, dataset, the last mattering most; the toolchain is the sibling books', so numbers can be checked.

Note One paragraph does three jobs (the pins, why the dataset pin matters, the toolchain and its sibling-book justification) and repeats the opening's renv paragraph.

Key Takeaways 194 words

Says
Five bullets restate the two models, the fixed-direction crossing, the three failures, the one guard, and the legacy exports.
Why here
Chapter contract; not stated.
  1. takeaway ¶1 Two data models are unavoidable: the long table to build, the matrix every analytics function consumes.
  2. takeaway ¶2 The boundary is crossed in a fixed direction, one call each way.
  3. takeaway ¶3 It fails three ways, none reported downstream: padding, reordering, duplicate keys.
  4. takeaway ¶4 The guard is always the same: index by name, then stopifnot() that the names agree.
  5. takeaway ¶5 The _v1/_v2 exports are shims; the package fetches no data.

Note ¶5 restates the callout and is the only takeaway not about the boundary.

Exercises 171 words

Says
Three exercises: the money markets across the boundary both ways, a ragged panel's covariance under two use options, and the legacy exports' modern counterparts.
Why here
Chapter contract; not stated.
  1. exercise ¶1 Attach the five money-market series to R by merge() and by bind_rows(); which route makes the alignment check unnecessary.
  2. exercise ¶2 Remove one asset's first three model years; compare complete and pairwise covariance, test positive definiteness, say which the package uses.
  3. exercise ¶3 For each legacy export, find whether an unsuffixed counterpart exists; what update_constraint_v1tov2() says about the interface change.

Note The exercises revisit the ragged case and the callout; neither column order nor duplicate keys, the other two failure modes, gets an exercise.

2 Notation and Data

5963 words · 6 sections · 59 paragraphs

Claim
Every chapter computes on the same four objects, on excess returns, fixed here once, and on one of two data sources: a modeled market with no real data on its own calendar, the default because it reproduces offline, and a free real-data layer with a pinned cutoff and a manifest, used only where a claim must be about real markets.
Question
What do the symbols mean and where do the numbers come from; cites Markowitz for needing the four objects and Coqueret and Scheuch et al. for opening a book this way.
Why here
Needs chapter 1 for as_xts_pm() and the tidy-to-xts crossing. Fixes the notation so that no later chapter redefines a symbol and describes the data so that no later chapter explains what it computes on; sits on neither side of the strategic–tactical distinction chapter 3 defines but is the ground both stand on. Chapter 9 takes the moments equation, every chapter the three-object data block and the calendar tools, chapters 4, 7, 15, 20, 21, 22 and the appendices the individual tables and caveats.
Does
Read any symbol in the book, build R, rf and Re, annualize two ways and say which applies, read a model-calendar date and an episode, name every shipped table and its trap, and fetch and check the real sleeves.
Order
Notation (with annualizing and the data block), the modeled market in seven subsections, the real-data layer, vocabulary, takeaways, exercises. The theory–implementation–application contract does not apply: a reference chapter with code interleaved as checks throughout and no application section.

Notation 1304 words

Says
Bold vectors and matrices, simple total returns, excess over the bill as the default basis, and the portfolio's mean and variance as the equation every later chapter starts from.
Why here
First because no later chapter may redefine a symbol; chapter 9 takes the moments equation as its start, and chapter 3 supplies the argument for the excess basis that is only stated here.
  1. definition ¶1 Typographic conventions, N assets over T periods, simple total return defined; simple over log because of cross-asset linearity.
  2. definition ¶2 The bill is the risk-free rate; excess returns the default basis everywhere; chapter 3 argues why, here only stated.
  3. definition ¶3 Weights, the ones vector, μ and Σ with hats for estimates; portfolio mean and variance; Sharpe ratio needs no subtraction.

Note ¶2 says the reasons for the excess basis belong to chapter 3 and then lists three of them; it half-argues what it says it will not argue.

The Modeled Market 2848 words

Says
The default data is a modeled market with no real data, on its own calendar, shipped inside tidypm so that every number reproduces offline; the section lists its tables, says how the series are built, and names the properties a reader must know before modeling on it.

Notation says what is computed, this says what it is computed on; the calendar precedes the tables because every date in them reads only on it; the caveats come last because they follow from how the series are built.

Three caveats, one departure, and a frozen version 763 words

Says
Currency dominates foreign bonds, the bond block is nearly rank-deficient at instrument level and the equities are single names; euro-area equity carries a deliberate three-point overlay within one standard error; and the dataset is frozen at one version because regenerating redraws the paths.
Why here
The three properties are consequences of how the market is built, so they follow the construction, and a reader should know them before modeling on it; the freeze closes the description.
  1. transition ¶1 Three properties follow from the construction; know them before modeling.
  2. caveat ¶2 Currency dominates foreign bonds: hedging removes most of their dollar volatility; only US and GLOBAL are free of it.
  3. caveat ¶3 Three maturities on a three-factor curve and two grades on one credit factor make the instrument covariance practically singular.
  4. code conditioning
  5. result ¶4 The instrument condition number makes unconstrained mean-variance nonsense there; the sleeves shrink or constrain; no problem at class level.
  6. caveat ¶5 Equity series are single securities; a portfolio of them diversifies more readily than a real index would.
  7. claim ¶6 One deliberate departure: a three-point drift on euro-area equity, a capital-market assumption, stated as a parameter.
  8. code overlay
  9. result ¶7 Without it EU_equity is a dead column in the tangency portfolio; the overlay is within one standard error.
  10. caveat ¶8 Frozen at version 6.2.0: a regenerated export is a different path; a script names the numbers that moved.

Note The overlay chunk builds and solves the long-only tangency portfolio of chapter 9 to justify a data choice, the chapter's heaviest chunk and the only place a reader meets optimize.portfolio() before chapter 9; the heading counts three caveats and the freeze in ¶8 is in practice a fourth.

3 Return, Risk, and the Investor's Problem

7987 words · 5 sections · 76 paragraphs

Claim
The investor's problem is expected utility, approximated by μ_w − (γ/2)σ²_w on returns in excess of the bill, and the approximation is only as good as its conventions for return, horizon, currency and riskless alternative, which are fixed here once.
Question
What does an investor want from a portfolio, and in which units is the answer measured; @markowitz1952 for the first, @vonneumann1944 for the second.
Why here
Needs the sixteen series and symbols of chapter 2 and the environment of chapter 1; says the definitions are fixed here so that no later chapter revisits them; chapter 4 measures the bill against the money markets and the currency split, 9 derives the frontier from the dominance vocabulary, 11 adds moments to the criterion, 16 onward carry the tactical label, 25 applies the reporting rule.
Does
State the objective on excess returns with a γ read in basis points, pass it to PortfolioAnalytics as γ/2, annualize means and Sharpe ratios in a named convention, and sort series into dominated and efficient before any optimizer runs.
Order
Opening, theory in ten subsections, implementation in six, application in four, Key Takeaways, Exercises; the contract holds, except that theory already computes (three chunks, a γ table, an implied γ), the application derives the separation result, the last two theory subsections have no implementation or application counterpart, and there are six takeaways and four exercises.

Averages, annualization, and the variance drag 267 words

Says
The arithmetic mean is the input to every objective, the geometric mean is what was earned, and half the variance separates them.
Why here
Needs compounding from the previous subsection; says the application meets a series with the higher arithmetic and the lower geometric mean.
  1. definition ¶1 Two averages answer different questions; drag ≈ σ²/2; small for a bond, not for equity; rankings can cross.
  2. definition ¶2 Annualization of μ, σ and g; the first two from independence; two mistakes from mixing them.

Note ¶2 holds a definition and two caveats (geometric times twelve, √12 under autocorrelation) in one paragraph.

Excess returns as the basis 395 words

Says
Every optimized return is in excess of the Treasury bill, for four reasons of rising consequence, and the bill is not a money-market series.
Why here
Reason two needs the Sharpe ratio just defined; the application measures reason three; chapter 4 measures the money-market gap.
  1. claim ¶1 Excess return defined; four reasons follow in increasing order of consequence.
  2. evidence ¶2 Tangency portfolio and market equilibrium exist only relative to a riskless rate (Tobin, Sharpe).
  3. caveat ¶3 PortfolioAnalytics has no riskless-rate argument; excess returns make its ratio the Sharpe ratio.
  4. claim ¶4 An uninvested balance earns zero in the objective, true only for excess returns; the application measures it.
  5. claim ¶5 The bill moves, so only the excess formulation is consistent through time.
  6. code bill-path
  7. result ¶6 The bill's first and lowest annual rates; comparing against zero is wrong in at least one.
  8. caveat ¶7 The riskless rate is the bill, not the money markets, which carry duration; chapter 4 measures.

Note The first theory subsection to read a number from a chunk; the implementation has not started.

Currency 201 words

Says
Every series is an unhedged dollar return, and hedging is one subtraction of the matching currency return.
Why here
Not stated; the implementation applies the subtraction to the foreign governments and chapter 4 decomposes the variance.
  1. definition ¶1 Dollar return compounds local return and currency return; hedged return is the difference.

The investor's objective 839 words

Says
Expected utility expands to μ_w − (γ/2)σ²_w, a certainty equivalent whose γ is a price with units an investor can read.
Why here
The opening says the problem is stated after the basis; the wealth equation puts the balance in the bill, which needs the basis; dominance and the package call need the criterion.
  1. definition ¶1 Wealth, weights, balance in the bill; maximize expected utility of terminal wealth (von Neumann–Morgenstern).
  2. definition ¶2 Concavity is risk aversion; Arrow–Pratt coefficients; premium ≈ ½γσ².
  3. derivation ¶3 Second-order expansion gives the mean-variance criterion; Markowitz's exactness conditions; Levy–Markowitz evidence; chapter 11 adds moments.
  4. derivation ¶4 Two readings: a certainty equivalent, and γ as the price of variance along an indifference curve.
  5. example ¶5 The price in units: volatility accepted for one point at γ = 2 and 10; cost of ten percent at γ = 4.
  6. code tbl-03-gamma
  7. claim ¶6 The table to keep in mind; customary range two to ten; an all-equity investor's implied γ by the first-order condition.
  8. code implied-gamma
  9. result ¶7 The implied γ on this sample.
  10. claim ¶8 CRRA as the alternative; equivalent to mean-variance in log returns under lognormality; tails differ, chapter 11.

Note Longest subsection; eight paragraphs and two chunks run from derivation through a units table to the CRRA alternative, which could stand alone.

The horizon of the problem 160 words

Says
Under independent returns the long-lived investor is myopic, so the one-period problem re-solved at each rebalancing is exact; changing opportunities add hedging demands the book does not model.
Why here
Answers the objection that the objective is one-period; refers forward to the horizon axis defined below.
  1. caveat ¶1 Samuelson–Merton myopia; hedging demands when opportunities move (Campbell 2002); the book's myopic policy; horizon chapters are about inputs.

Dominance, efficiency, and the northwest rule 219 words

Says
A dominates B with at least as much return and at most as much risk, one strict; a better allocation lies northwest; what nothing dominates is efficient.
Why here
Follows from having two moments per portfolio; says the vocabulary applies before γ is chosen and every later chapter uses it; chapter 9 derives the frontier.
  1. definition ¶1 Dominance in the volatility–return plane.
  2. definition ¶2 The northwest rule; efficient; weaker than the utility criterion (a partial order) and stronger (holds for any γ).

Strategic and tactical allocation 440 words

Says
Strategic is the long-run policy from unconditional moments, tactical a deviation on conditioning information; the chapters are sorted, and a portfolio is judged by the measure it was chosen by.
Why here
The opening says the chapter closes by defining the terms once; the sort needs the objective, which the strategic chapters solve from full-sample moments.
  1. claim ¶1 Two cuts: granularity orders the parts; horizon needs two terms defined here.
  2. definition ¶2 Strategic and tactical defined; orthogonal to granularity; Brinson, Ibbotson, Sharpe 1987, Ang, DeMiguel.
  3. transition ¶3 Chapters 9–15 strategic, 16–19 tactical, 23–25 implementation and evaluation; each chapter says which.
  4. claim ¶4 The measure judged by must be the measure chosen by; Artzner, Acerbi; chapters 10 and 25.

Note ¶4 is a separate rule under a heading about horizon; neither this subsection nor the previous has an implementation or application counterpart.

The northwest rule applied 790 words

Says
Most of the sixteen series are dominated on the arithmetic excess basis; the efficient set is the same on the compounded basis, but who dominates whom moves with the drag.
Why here
Says the application opens with the picture and that the count needs no optimizer; chapter 9 draws the same picture on compounded total returns.
  1. method ¶1 One point per series, arithmetic excess mean vertical, one data frame so no mismatched pairs.
  2. code moments
  3. code tbl-03-dominance
  4. code fig-03-northwest
  5. result ¶2 Number dominated; the efficient set; the most-dominated series; one sample, no test; chapter 9 picks the combination.
  6. claim ¶3 On compounded total returns the efficient set holds and the relation beneath it changes.
  7. code basis-dominance
  8. result ¶4 Same efficient series; some gain a dominator; the largest-drag case's lead is smaller than its drag; both statements true.

4 Descriptive Analysis of Asset Returns

4740 words · 5 sections · 47 paragraphs

Claim
Volatility is the only moment this market lets us estimate with confidence, and the two things that shape every portfolio, tails that arrive as market-wide events and a currency share that belongs to the asset rather than the country, are invisible in a table of moments.
Question
Which of the known regularities of financial returns hold in the modeled market, and which of the numbers the book is about to rely on can be estimated at all; cited to Mandelbrot (1963), Fama (1965) and Cont (2001).
Why here
Every later chapter does something to a return series, the text says, and none of that is interpretable or trustworthy without this one; it takes the modeled market from chapter 2 and the tidy-to-matrix boundary from chapter 1, and it says the mean–variance asymmetry splits chapters 6 and 7, that chapter 10 settles the risk-measure question, chapter 15 the hedge per sleeve, chapter 25 the per-episode reporting, and that Part IV's optimizers are bounded by its two properties.
Does
Compute and read the annualized moments on the modeled market, test normality, compare two expected-shortfall estimators, judge each moment on rolling windows, read drawdown episodes, count joint exceedances, compare the four episodes, and split a foreign series' variance into local and currency parts.
Order
Theory, implementation, application, takeaways, exercises, as the contract has it; but the implementation reads every result it computes and carries both tables and two of the three figures, so it is the first application, the section titled as the application holds the chapter's only derivation outside the theory (@eq-04-fx), and the mean–volatility picture closes the implementation's first subsection instead of opening the application.

The mean is the moment that does not improve 190 words

Says
The mean's precision depends on the calendar span, the variance's on the number of observations; the higher moments are worse.
Why here
It rates the moments just defined; the text says this asymmetry is what splits chapter 6 from chapter 7, and the rolling-window subsection later makes it concrete.
  1. derivation ¶1 Under independence, standard errors of mean and variance over span τ at interval Δ (@eq-04-precision).
  2. claim ¶2 Finer sampling helps the variance, not the mean; Merton's point; why chapters 6 and 7 differ in difficulty.
  3. claim ¶3 Skewness and kurtosis are worse: one extreme observation can outweigh the rest of the sample.

Testing normality rather than assuming it 245 words

Says
Jarque–Bera tests normality; whether it matters depends on the quantity, and for the tail the Cornish–Fisher correction prices the normal assumption.
Why here
Not stated; it supplies the statistic and the two expected-shortfall estimators that the implementation's distribution test applies.
  1. definition ¶1 The Jarque–Bera statistic (@eq-04-jb); a specification test, not a model; rejection says only that shape exceeds sampling error.
  2. definition ¶2 Normality hardly matters for variance, decides a tail quantile; VaR and ES (@eq-04-es); ES preferred as coherent (Artzner, Acerbi).
  3. definition ¶3 ES from the sample tail or a Cornish–Fisher quantile (@eq-04-cornish-fisher; Favre, Boudt); the package default; the gap prices normality.

Note ¶2 both defines two risk measures and argues the preference for one; the Sharpe ratio and maximum drawdown, two columns of the next table, are defined nowhere in this section.

Key Takeaways 265 words

Says
Six bullets: the moment asymmetry in theory and on rolling windows, non-normality apart from volatility, tails as events, episodes not one crisis, currency as an asset property.
Why here
Not stated (contract position).
  1. takeaway ¶1 The four moments differ in kind: mean precision from the calendar span, variance from the observation count (Merton).
  2. takeaway ¶2 On rolling windows volatility never flips sign and moves little; the mean moves more than its level; skewness reverses often.
  3. takeaway ¶3 Non-normality and volatility are unrelated: all series reject, JP equity nearly Gaussian, US credit's two ES estimates far apart.
  4. takeaway ¶4 Fat tails are events: joint exceedances independence would produce once in many samples; shared tails do not diversify.
  5. takeaway ¶5 The four episodes are not one crisis; correlation rises in two, falls in the equity bear; short-window rises partly mechanical (Forbes).
  6. takeaway ¶6 The currency share belongs to the asset: hedging the Japanese bond removes most volatility, Japanese equity none (Campbell).

Note Six bullets against the contract's "around five"; the money-market finding and the drawdown subsection have no bullet, and bullets 1 and 2 state one result twice, once in theory and once in measurement.

5 Factor Models and Beta Estimation

4429 words · 5 sections · 37 paragraphs

Claim
Most of what a single-factor regression appears to explain about this market does not survive proper measurement: the model fits only the equity blocks, its betas are mostly United States betas, the up/down split survives inference in two cases of sixteen, non-equity betas change sign in about a third of windows, and a global size factor sorts on region.
Question
What do the sixteen series have in common? Cites Sharpe, Lintner and Black for the model and Jensen for the regression form.
Why here
Stated as the continuation of chapter 4, which described the market one series at a time; takes the excess-return basis from chapter 3 and the instrument universe from chapter 2 for the proxy. Forward: chapter 8 is said to exist because a small beta's sign is unknown, chapter 19's regime allocation is said to need the turbulence index, and the appendix carries the package note. Why factor models precede the input estimation of the next part is not stated.
Does
Build a lagged capitalization-weighted market proxy, estimate alphas and betas with Newey-West errors, test an up/down split, construct and inspect a size factor, roll betas, and compute an out-of-sample turbulence index.

The Single-Factor Model 417 words

Says
The CAPM regression splits each asset's variance into a market part and a residual, so R² is the share of risk the model describes; two extensions let beta differ by market direction and move over time.
Why here
Stated in the opening: the model is set out before it is estimated. Needs the excess-return basis of chapter 3; supplies the four equations the implementation estimates and the table is read through.
  1. definition ¶1 Notation; excess returns throughout, per chapter 3; the regression (eq-05-capm) and the population beta (eq-05-beta).
  2. derivation ¶2 Alpha is zero under the theory, a residual as description; the variance split and R² (eq-05-variance-split).
  3. claim ¶3 R² is not decoration: it is the share of risk the model claims; low R² means nothing to say.
  4. definition ¶4 Two extensions: an up/down beta (Bawa-Lindenberg, Ang et al.; eq-05-asymmetric) and a time-varying beta (conditional CAPM), done by rolling windows.

Note ¶4 defines both extensions in one paragraph; the second gets one sentence and no equation.

Inference is where these models are usually lost 130 words

Says
OLS standard errors are wrong for monthly returns, so Newey-West errors are used throughout, and sixteen repeated tests need them because repetition manufactures significance.
Why here
Stated: eq-05-asymmetric is estimated sixteen times, so the estimator is fixed before the first regression runs.
  1. method ¶1 Serial dependence and heteroskedasticity break OLS errors; the Newey-West estimator (eq-05-neweywest) with six lags is used throughout.
  2. claim ¶2 Sixteen repetitions will produce apparent differences regardless; Harvey, Liu and Zhu's argument applies to a table of betas.

Splitting the beta, and then testing the split 635 words

Says
Up- and down-market betas can differ in sign where the unconditional beta is zero, but only two of sixteen splits survive a Newey-West test, and the credit one is not among them.

  6. code asymmetry-test
  7. code asymmetry-claims

A beta is a time series 610 words

  3. code tbl-05-rolling
  4. code rolling-claims
  5. transition ¶2 Two patterns emerge; the second is the one to carry forward.
  6. result ¶3 The furthest-traveling betas are the smallest; US_equity near one barely moves and never flips (Blume).
  7. result ¶4 Every non-equity beta changes sign, the median in about a third of windows; US_government alone reliably negative.
  8. code fig-05-rolling-betas
  9. claim ¶5 A small beta has an unknown sign; hence chapter 8, and a hedge ratio needs an error bar first.

Note ¶2 announces two patterns that ¶3 and ¶4 then carry; the figure follows the reading of the table rather than opening the section.

Key Takeaways 252 words

7 The Covariance Matrix

5002 words · 5 sections · 58 paragraphs

Claim
On more assets than observations the sample covariance matrix is singular by arithmetic; of the four replacements, shrinkage is the one that always runs but earns nothing once the window is long, and the long-only constraint was already doing comparable work.
Question
Why the lowest-risk combination of 123 equities cannot be obtained from the monthly history available; @michaud1989 for the consequence and @chopra1993 for its price.
Why here

Does

What the Optimizer Requires 999 words

Says
The optimizer's only input is Σ, whose sample estimate has rank at most min(N, T−1), so with more assets than observations it is singular whatever the data.
Why here
stated in the opening's plan: first what the optimizer asks for and why the request cannot be met.
  1. definition ¶1 Minimum variance minimizes w'Σw; the matrix is the optimizer's entire input, nothing else about the assets enters.
  2. definition ¶2 A symmetric Σ holds N(N+1)/2 numbers, estimated from T observations by the sample formula (eq-07-sample).
  3. derivation ¶3 A sum of T rank-one outer products has rank at most min(N, T−1) (eq-07-rank).
  4. claim ¶4 With N > T−1 the estimate is singular whatever the data; arithmetic, not a property of markets.
  5. evidence ¶5 Sixteen classes need 136 numbers; 123 equities need thousands from 372 months, more parameters than observations.

What singularity claims about portfolios 316 words

Says
A singular estimate asserts a riskless long-short portfolio, which a minimizer will hold; short of singularity the condition number says how far sampling error is magnified into the weights.
Why here
stated: eq-07-rank is a statement about a matrix, this subsection turns it into a statement about money.
  1. derivation ¶1 A singular estimate admits a non-zero portfolio with zero variance (eq-07-riskless); a minimizer finds it and holds it.
  2. claim ¶2 Such portfolios are long-short cancellations peculiar to the estimation months; @michaud1989's "error maximizer" is exact.
  3. definition ¶3 Short of singularity the condition number (eq-07-condition) governs error magnification; the smallest eigenvalues are noise, @laloux1999, @marchenko1967.
  4. evidence ¶4 @chopra1993 rank the errors: means, then variances, then covariances; why chapter 6 is harder and this chapter unavoidable.

Diagnosing it before the solver does 399 words

Says
Rank, riskless directions and condition number flag the problem without an inverse; the equities are singular on five years, and the full instrument universe is ill-conditioned even with N < T.
Why here
needs the rank bound and the condition number just defined; the reason for diagnosing before solving is in the title, no other reason is given.
  1. method ¶1 Two numbers, neither requiring an inverse.
  2. code diagnose
  3. result ¶2 Rank below assets means singular; riskless_directions counts free portfolios; five years on the equities yields dozens.
  4. caveat ¶3 A finite but huge condition number is more dangerous than an infinite one; past 10^8 the inverse reads noise.
  5. result ¶4 All instruments on the full sample: N < T−1, yet rank deficient, condition 10^12; the bond block nearly replicates itself.
  6. code diagnose-claims
  7. code rank-by-level
  8. result ¶5 The singular rows show a negative smallest eigenvalue: floating-point noise around zero, a world with negative variance.

Note ¶3 defines the condition number a second time, two paragraphs after eq-07-condition did; the hidden chunk asserts ¶4's numbers.

Route two: impose a structure 412 words

Says
A k-factor structure replaces N(N+1)/2 parameters with Nk + N and cannot be singular; six lines do it, while the package's version refuses exactly the windows where it is needed.
Why here
next in the cost order; takes the parameter count from the theory section as its motivation.
  1. definition ¶1 Factor structure (eq-07-factor): Nk + N numbers, never singular since D is positive; @fan2008, @bai2002, @connor1993 for convergence and k.
  2. method ¶2 Factors may be economic, as in chapter 5, or statistical principal components; the latter is six lines.
  3. code factor-covariance
  4. result ¶3 Full rank and a finite condition number where the sample matrix has dozens of riskless directions; structure does the work.
  5. transition ¶4 The package ships this as statistical.factor.model(), with one constraint worth knowing.
  6. code factor-model-package
  7. caveat ¶5 The package function demands T ≥ N, exactly when unneeded; the body does not; appendix D records the patch.

Route three: shrink the matrix 205 words

Says
Pull the sample matrix toward a well-conditioned target with a data-chosen λ; it needs no assumption, cannot fail and is always invertible.
Why here
next in the cost order; not otherwise stated.
  1. definition ¶1 Shrinkage (eq-07-shrinkage) pulls the sample toward a well-conditioned target; @stein1956, @ledoit2003, @ledoit2004, @schafer2005, @ledoit2017 attributed.
  2. claim ¶2 The workhorse: no assumption about what drives returns, cannot fail, invertible whenever F is and λ > 0.
  3. code shrunk
  4. result ¶3 lambda is the weight on the target, here a stated share of the way toward independence.

Route four: estimate robustly 38 words

Says

8 Estimation Error and Its Cost

7979 words · 5 sections · 51 paragraphs

Claim
What an input costs is set by how well it can be estimated; the mean cannot be, so the rule that estimates it promises most and delivers least, while the rules that estimate only the covariance keep most of their promise and lead the register.
Question
How much is a better forecast worth, in Sharpe ratio; the opening cites @demiguel2009 for asking it the other way round (none of fourteen optimizing rules beat 1/N reliably), with the line from @jobson1980 to @ledoit2004 behind it.
Why here
The text says ch. 6 and ch. 7 estimate the mean and the covariance separately and this chapter measures what they cost together and which costs more; it takes the objective from ch. 3, the tangency and minimum-variance solutions and their quadratic programs from ch. 9 one chapter ahead, the precision result from ch. 4, the condition number from ch. 7, and the rolling scheme and the register from ch. 25; it says ch. 26 and ch. 27 take the forecast-error measure from it, ch. 14 treats inverse volatility in its own right, and ch. 19 and ch. 28 take up the drift it cannot price.
Does
Measure a rule's Sharpe gap on rolling windows against the equal-weight control; split an estimated portfolio's loss between the mean and the covariance by substitution under a simulated truth; locate the window at which a plug-in rule overtakes 1/N on a given universe; call the two naive rules directly rather than through the rebalancing loop.
Order
Opening (question, roadmap, data), theory (the plug-in rule, the two naive rules, inflation and expected loss with a worked callout, the gap defined), implementation (the naive rules and their trap, the two quadratic programs, the rolling loop with a cross-check against the register, the gap table and figure), application (a simulated truth, the two channels, forecast error in the investor's units, the crossover region, the register line), takeaways, exercises. Deviations: the theory section takes its portfolios and programs from ch. 9 and derives nothing itself; the application opens with a method paragraph and three numbers, not with a picture; the chapter's first result arrives at the end of the implementation section; the forecast-error subsection is a one-paragraph pointer to Part VIII placed between the channel split and the crossover; seven takeaways and four exercises.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. "Cutting edge research", very good, and good that it works on the modeled market. Raised together with the question whether the level fits a master's student or a practitioner.

IVPortfolio Optimization

Says
The textbook frontier and how far resampling moves it (9), then five variations: another risk measure (10), more moments (11), constraints (12), solvers (13), risk instead of capital (14). Two of the five change little (10, 11); the other three say the answer depends on constraints, convexity and an audit.
Why here
Chapter 9 calls itself the point every later method departs from; chapters 10, 13 and 14 each give one opening sentence ("every portfolio so far…"). Why constraints (12) come before the solvers (13) whose audit they borrow is not stated.
Note
Chapter 9 runs to 10,239 words, 4,889 of them application, and points to a callout that is not there. Chapter 12's implementation is a catalogue of ten constraint families, 47 percent of the chapter. Chapters 13 and 14 are less than half as long as their neighbors.

9 Mean-Variance Optimization

10239 words · 5 sections · 101 paragraphs

Claim
The textbook frontier on the sixteen series is a sequence of quadratic programs whose left end is set by the least volatile asset rather than by diversification, whose right end moves several times as far as the left when the sample is resampled, and whose tangency portfolio keeps the smallest share of its promised Sharpe ratio out of sample while minimum variance, which needs no μ, keeps the most.
Question
How should an investor divide wealth across assets that differ in expected return, volatility and comovement; @markowitz1952.
Why here
Says Markowitz is the point every later method departs from; needs the sixteen series of chapters 2 and 4, the excess basis and dominance vocabulary of chapter 3, the factor finding of chapter 5, the rolling moments of chapter 4 and the estimated inputs of chapters 6 and 7 (chapter 8, on estimation error, is never cited); chapter 10 changes the risk measure, 11 adds moments, 12 prices constraints, 13 the solvers, 15 shrinkage and universe choice, 23 the turnover, 25 the honest out-of-sample version, Part VIII the robust formulations.
Does
Specify and solve minimum-variance, tangency and quadratic-utility problems with a guarded solver call, draw frontier, weights and capital allocation line from one data frame, and test a frontier for stability, source of volatility reduction, constraint cost and out-of-sample record.
Order
Opening, theory in two subsections (one carrying a twelve-paragraph worked callout), implementation in five, application in seven, Key Takeaways, Exercises; the headings keep the contract, but the implementation already reads results (the dominance count, the US_credit asymmetry), the application is half the chapter and runs five experiments on the frontier rather than showing it, and there are six takeaways and five exercises.

The Mean-Variance Problem 1647 words

Says
Portfolio variance carries the covariances, dominance gives the frontier a direction, and the problem has two closed-form anchors and a closed-form frontier that vanish under a short-sale constraint and mislead once the inputs are estimates.
Why here
The opening puts derivation first; says the dominance vocabulary is applied in the implementation before any optimizer runs; its last paragraph hands the rest of the chapter to implementation and application.
  1. definition ¶1 Symbols w, μ, Σ, ι; portfolio mean and variance (eq-09-moments).
  2. claim ¶2 The variance line is Markowitz; off-diagonals enter and can be negative; a portfolio can undercut every constituent.
  3. definition ¶3 Dominance in the plane, from chapter 3; northwest rule; efficient; applies to single assets, implementation does so.
  4. definition ¶4 For each target return, the least-variance fully invested portfolio (eq-09-problem).
  5. definition ¶5 Traced over targets: the minimum-variance frontier; upper branch efficient, lower branch never chosen.

Note ¶3 restates chapter 3's definition in full before the problem is posed, as the house rule asks.

Two anchors with closed forms 1069 words

Says
Minimum variance needs no μ, the tangency portfolio is Tobin's single risky fund with the highest Sharpe ratio, and Merton's constants give the whole frontier.
Why here
Drops the return constraint from the problem just posed; says μ's absence will do much work later; the two-asset callout follows the formula it reduces; the tangency and the frontier need the risk-free asset Tobin adds.
  1. derivation ¶1 Dropping the return constraint gives the minimum-variance portfolio (eq-09-gmv).
  2. claim ¶2 μ is absent; the one frontier point needing no return view; will do work later.
  3. derivation ¶3 (callout, collapsed) With two assets the formula collapses to a scalar expression (eq-09-two-asset).
  4. example callout ¶4 US_equity and US_government, one volatile and one not; their annualized sample moments.
  5. code two-asset-inputs
  6. example callout ¶5 Moments substituted into the weight formula, then into the portfolio volatility.
  7. method callout ¶6 The same arithmetic in code, so the numbers are computed, not typed.
  8. code two-asset-weights
  9. result callout ¶7 Portfolio volatility below either asset; diversification in one number; the cross term subtracts.
  10. method callout ¶8 Vary the correlation with both volatilities fixed.
  11. code two-asset-rho
  12. result callout ¶9 At ρ = ±1 volatility is zero, short at +1; benefit shrinks as ρ rises; measured ρ leaves little.
  13. claim callout ¶10 Two lessons: a singular Σ gives a zero-variance combination (eq-07-riskless); the closed form shorts, here as on sixteen.
  14. method callout ¶11 Scalar and matrix formula must agree.
  15. code two-asset-check
  16. caveat callout ¶12 The stopifnot() guards against drift and fails the render.
  17. derivation ¶13 Tobin: with a risk-free asset every efficient portfolio mixes it with one risky fund, the tangency portfolio (eq-09-tangency).
  18. evidence ¶14 Highest Sharpe ratio (Sharpe 1966); lets Sharpe 1964 reach equilibrium; Roll 1977 on identifying it.
  19. derivation ¶15 Merton's A, B, C give the frontier (eq-09-frontier); vertex 1/A; maximum Sharpe √C; proofs in appendix B.

Note The callout sits between the two anchors the title promises, so the tangency portfolio arrives twelve paragraphs after the first; ¶10 reads a sixteen-asset number before the implementation has begun.

Why the closed forms are not what we compute 236 words

Says
A short-sale constraint turns the problem into a convex quadratic program, and estimated inputs make the closed forms error-maximizers.
Why here
Says every expression above assumed unconstrained weights; frames the rest: compute the textbook answer in the implementation, measure how much is real in the application.
  1. claim ¶1 Short-sale constraint removes the closed form; a convex QP solved in milliseconds (Boyd); chapter 13 for machinery.
  2. evidence ¶2 μ and Σ are estimates (chapters 6, 7); Jobson–Korkie, Best–Grauer's mechanism, Chopra–Ziemba's price, Michaud's error-maximizer.
  3. transition ¶3 That literature frames the chapter: compute, then measure.

Key Takeaways 220 words

Says
Six bullets: the quadratic program, the μ-free anchor, concentration, the two ends, the universe, the out-of-sample record.
Why here
Contract position.
  1. takeaway ¶1 Closed form only unconstrained; a short-sale constraint makes a quadratic program.
  2. takeaway ¶2 Minimum variance has no μ; no forecast needed; far more stable than tangency.
  3. takeaway ¶3 Concentration is not a symptom; a dominant factor produces it with perfect inputs (Green–Hollifield, Clarke).
  4. takeaway ¶4 Left end Σ, right end μ; the bootstrap moves tangency several times as far.
  5. takeaway ¶5 The level is set by the least volatile asset; universe selection over weighting machinery.
  6. takeaway ¶6 In sample tangency first; out of sample it keeps least; behind equal weight at 60 months, not 120 (DeMiguel, Jagannathan–Ma).

10 Risk Measures Beyond Variance

7637 words · 5 sections · 52 paragraphs

Claim
Replacing variance by expected shortfall changes little on sixteen class series: the two frontiers are one curve within the width of the line, the minimum-ES portfolio swaps one holding and moves one moment, and out of sample the tail rule realizes the higher shortfall because a 5% tail on sixty months is three observations.
Question
What changes when variance is replaced by a measure that is subadditive; cites Markowitz (1959) for the semi-variance and Artzner et al. (1999) for coherence in the first two sentences.
Why here
Opens on chapter 9's optimizers, which all used variance; takes the data from chapter 2, the excess basis, the strategic label, quadratic utility and the "judge by the measure chosen" rule from chapter 3, the ES definition, the Cornish-Fisher expansion, the ellipticity verdict and the credit non-normality from chapter 4, the problem, the frontier and the US_credit holding from chapter 9, and γ/2 from appendix B; hands the comoments to 11 ("here they enter only through the measure"), the convex-versus-search distinction and the ratio normalization to 13, the ES decomposition to 14, the register to 25, the cost of a tail estimate to 26, and two upstream issues to the package notes. That one clause is the only reason given for preceding chapter 11; why it precedes 12: not stated.
Does
Test subadditivity on every pair of a panel; pose minimum ES as the Rockafellar-Uryasev linear program under ROI; read any weight vector on every measure with extract_risk(); trace two frontiers from one data frame; name the solver each objective needs; set the ratio flags explicitly; recognize an unevaluated CSM and a wrong-sign EQS; read the register with an error bar.
Order
Theory in six subsections (coherence, value at risk, expected shortfall and its linear program, the modified measure, downside and drawdown and two more coherent measures, what each measure assumes), implementation in three (the four specifications and the package defaults, the mean-ES frontier, CSM and EQS under CVXR), application in three (four portfolios on one table, the cross table, the register), five takeaways, four exercises. Deviations: the theory runs code in two collapsed worked boxes; all three figures and the chapter's central reading (the US_credit weight along the two frontiers and the one-month skewness behind it) sit in the implementation, so the application opens on a table rather than a picture; none of the three content sections has prose of its own above its subsections; the fourth of the four portfolios is produced by a chunk whose only introduction is inside a callout about a different trap.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Would fit better in the first content part; more precisely, the measures themselves belong there and the portfolio application stays where it is now.

Coherence, Tails, and What Variance Assumes 1879 words

Says
A risk measure must be subadditive to respect diversification; value at risk is not, expected shortfall is and is a linear program, the modified version is not convex, and every measure encodes a belief about the investor.
Why here
The opening's first clause ("We first set out the coherence axioms"); the subsections follow the opening's list in order. Paragraphs: none of its own.

Note Heading only; six subsections, two of which run code in collapsed boxes that later prose and the first takeaway depend on.

Four properties of a risk measure 229 words

Says
Coherence is four axioms, subadditivity is the one that matters for building portfolios, and variance is used despite failing it because under elliptical returns the choice of measure changes no ranking.
Why here
The opening's question is about "that property", so it is defined first; needs chapter 4's verdict that this market is not elliptical; ¶2's "the last axiom is the one that matters here" sets the test the next subsection applies.
  1. definition ¶1 X and ρ(X) in loss units; monotonicity, translation invariance, positive homogeneity, subadditivity; eq-10-subadditive.
  2. claim ¶2 Subadditivity is diversification; variance and standard deviation fail; under elliptical returns every such measure is a function of σ (McNeil); chapter 4 said not elliptical.

Value at risk 450 words

Says
Value at risk is the regulatory standard, satisfies three axioms and fails subadditivity, on this data for a few credit-government pairs of the same currency.
Why here
Named in the opening as the standard that lacks the property; the text says the failure is "not only in contrived examples", so the box tests it on every pair; the box's "expected shortfall, defined below, fails on none" hands over.
  1. definition ¶1 VaR_α = −q_α, eq-10-var; α = 0.05 throughout, p = 0.95 in the package; Jorion; fails eq-10-subadditive because a quantile ignores what lies beyond it.
  2. method ¶2 (callout, collapsed) Test every pair equally weighted; homogeneity makes it one comparison per pair; invert = FALSE returns losses as positives.
  3. code subadditivity
  4. code subadditivity-facts hidden; asserts no ES violation and that every VaR violation is a credit series with its own government bond

11 Higher Moments

7885 words · 5 sections · 59 paragraphs

Claim
On the sixteen classes the third and fourth moments move a γ = 5 investor out of credit and into government bonds, the direction the theory predicts, but the gain is a few basis points of certainty equivalent, smaller than the error an unpolished metaheuristic leaves and than the estimation uncertainty of the tensors; and the structured estimator that tames that uncertainty removes the very skewness the tensors were wanted for.
Question
What does an investor lose by describing an asset class with two moments when its returns are skewed and fat-tailed; cites Kraus–Litzenberger (1976) in the second sentence, Harvey–Siddique (2000) and Dittmar (2002) after it.
Why here
Declares itself strategic in chapter 3's sense; takes the expected-utility expansion and CRRA from chapter 3, the shape moments, Cornish–Fisher, expected shortfall and Jarque–Bera from chapter 4, the equity factor from chapter 5, the momentFUN route from chapters 6 and 7, the case for structure from chapter 7, the Kan–Zhou loss from chapter 8, the problem, tangency, minimum variance, frontier weights and block bootstrap from chapter 9, and expected shortfall as a risk measure from chapter 10; hands the solver shortfall to chapter 13, conditional dependence to chapter 19, the cost of estimation to chapters 26 and 28, and the convex solvers' moment behavior to appendix D. Why it sits between chapters 10 and 12 inside the part: not stated beyond taking expected shortfall from chapter 10.
Does
Compute coskewness and cokurtosis tensors and contract them to portfolio moments; fit the single-factor structured estimator and decompose what it drops; write a four-moment CRRA objective as a custom PortfolioAnalytics objective, supply tensors through momentFUN, and assert the objective was evaluated; polish a metaheuristic answer with slsqp() and price the solver error as a certainty equivalent; bootstrap a tensor element; measure tail dependence against a Gaussian copula.
Order
Opening with a worked case (credit's shape and its mean-variance weight), theory (expansion, tensors and their structure, CRRA, modified shortfall, copulas), implementation (sample tensors, structured estimator, moment interface, custom objective with two metaheuristics and a polishing box, modified shortfall under two estimators, conditional comoments), application (the shape picture, seven portfolios, bootstrap, tail dependence), takeaways, exercises. Deviations: the opening runs an optimizer and reads a weight before the theory; the implementation carries the chapter's first allocation finding (the shortfall comparison) and its verdict on the structured estimator, which the application re-reads; conditional comoments close the implementation without running and without a return; five takeaways, four exercises.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Already advanced, with the comoment tensors. No structural proposal; a remark on level for the stated audience.

12 Portfolio Constraints

6900 words · 5 sections · 55 paragraphs

Claim
On this universe the constraints are the precondition for a portfolio anyone would hold, not a refinement of the optimization: a binding line is the mandate correcting the estimator (Jagannathan–Ma), the package accepts every constraint but honors only some, and so every solved weight vector has to be audited against the specification it came from.
Question
How does an investment policy statement, written in prose by a committee, become something a solver can read; cites Jagannathan and Ma (2003) in the second sentence and Frost and Savarino (1988) in the third.
Why here
Declares itself strategic allocation in chapter 3's vocabulary and takes chapter 3's quadratic utility and γ/2 convention; the problem, the GMV equation, the two anchors and the group-by-name rule from chapter 9; the shrunk covariance from chapter 7, the estimation error from chapter 8, the betas from chapter 5; borrows chapter 13's maxSR verification, its four-line audit() and its measurement of heuristics as if already read. Hands the non-convex solver choice to 13, HHI() to 14, sleeves to 15, scores to 20, turnover to 23, the transfer coefficient and tracking error to 24, the trap record to the solver matrix and the package notes. Why it follows chapter 11 and precedes chapter 13 inside the part: not stated.
Does
Build every constraint family in PortfolioAnalytics, know which solver honors which, audit a weight vector line by line with audit_spec(), price each line of a mandate by finite differences, and repair a dropped position limit with a screen.
Order
Theory (feasible set, shadow price, Jagannathan–Ma, transfer coefficient), implementation (anchors and helpers, then one subsection per family, closed by the audit function), application (a mandate solved, audited, repaired, priced line by line, read up a ladder), takeaways, exercises. Deviations: the theory's central equation is verified in a collapsed box inside the implementation; the families are implemented in neither the order of tbl-12-families nor by convexity; four traps are callouts while the two return-target traps are plain prose; the application ends in a conclusion subsection; four exercises.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Sensible as it is.

The Jagannathan--Ma covariance 211 words

13 Numerical Optimization and Solvers

3315 words · 5 sections · 49 paragraphs

Claim
Convexity, not the solver, decides whether a weight vector is the answer: the convex solvers reach an independently computed optimum exactly, every search lands short of it and slower, and three of five solvers return a portfolio that violates a position limit they accepted without a word, so every result has to be audited against its request.
Question
Which of the routines behind optimize.portfolio() return the answer rather than a weight vector, and which honor the constraints they accept; cites nobody in the opening — the first citation (Boyd and Vandenberghe) arrives in the theory section's third paragraph.
Why here
Stated only as "every portfolio problem posed so far" ending in a numerical routine. Takes the singular-covariance case from chapter 7, the constraint set from chapter 12 and the budget-encoding failure from chapter 9; hands the accuracy band to chapter 14's risk budgeting (said to have no convex formulation in the package), HHI() and diversification() to chapter 14, the selection-bias parallel to chapter 25, the patch state to appendix 93. Why it follows 12 and precedes 14 inside the part is implied (12's constraints are what breaks convexity, 14's problem is what forces a heuristic) but never said.
Does

Convexity Is the Whole Question — 606 (260 before the subsections)

Says
The choice is between convex and non-convex problems, not between solvers; on a convex problem every local minimum is global, so converged means correct.
Why here
Stated in the opening: what distinguishes the two families is set out first. Needs the positive semi-definite Σ and the singular case of chapter 7; the race reads its result through eq-13-convex.
  1. claim ¶1 Two families, not nine solvers; what separates them is a property of the problem, not the software.
  2. definition ¶2 A convex set and a convex function; eq-13-convex.
  3. derivation ¶3 Minimum variance is convex however Σ is estimated; the simplex is convex; local minimum is global, so converged means correct.
  4. evidence ¶4 Goldfarb–Idnani active set under quadprog/ROI, operator splitting under OSQP/CVXR; Nocedal–Wright for both.

Note The heading is a sentence, not a noun phrase (so is the application's). ¶3 carries three points (objective convex despite estimation, feasible set convex, the global-minimum consequence) around a display equation; the chapter's first citation arrives here.

Where convexity is lost 183 words

Says
A cardinality constraint turns the feasible set into a union of faces, which needs integer variables; that is what the heuristics exist for, and nothing stops their misuse on convex problems.
Why here
Stated: not every mandate fits the simplex; draws on chapter 12's constraints; supplies eq-13-cardinality to the feasibility test and the misuse premise to the race.
  1. definition ¶1 Chapter 12's constraints mostly stay convex; cardinality eq-13-cardinality is a union of faces, needs binary variables, NP-hard.
  2. evidence ¶2 Differential evolution, particle swarm, simulated annealing attributed; no guarantee, coverage for certainty; Ardia brought DE in; Gilli.
  3. claim ¶3 Nothing stops a user applying the searches to a convex problem, and many published examples do.

Note ¶3 is the premise of the race in one sentence, with no example for the published misuse it asserts.
