Why the Searches Fall Short — 787 (341 before the subsections)

Says
A random search covers a sixteen-dimensional simplex poorly, so more draws close only part of the gap at a growing cost; use a heuristic only when the problem forces it, and report its output as an estimate.
Why here
Contract position; builds on the race's run() and band; hands the band to chapter 14, whose risk budgeting is said to force a search.
  1. claim ¶1 One minimum and every downhill path reaches it; a search substitutes coverage for certainty, and the space is large.
  2. claim ¶2 The simplex's volume shrinks with N while directions grow, so coverage falls geometrically and each draw buys less.
  3. code budget random at 20,000 and 100,000 draws; share and relative time
  4. code budget-claims hidden: gap narrowed, more than one point still open, time rose
  5. result ¶3 Fivefold draws cost multiples of the time and closed part of the gap; darts on a wall; Gilli: report the spread.
  6. claim ¶4 Use a metaheuristic only when forced and treat it as an estimate; chapter 14's risk budgeting is forced, at this band.

Note An argument with one experiment on the race's machinery, not a result on the modeled market; ¶1 restates the theory section's conclusion; ¶3 already makes Gilli's random-variable point that the next subsection is titled for; the band is read a second time.

A heuristic's answer is a random variable 258 words

Says
A search's output depends on the seed, so it is a distribution to report; three habits: record the seed, report the spread, start from the convex answer.
Why here
Builds on the single draw in tbl-13-race; otherwise not stated, and the parent's ¶3 has already made the point.
  1. claim ¶1 Convex solvers are deterministic; DEoptim, pso, GenSA start from a seeded random population, so the table's number is one draw.
  2. claim ¶2 Two specifications on one run each may differ only by seed; Gilli: ten or twenty runs, quote a range.
  3. transition ¶3 Three habits follow, none costing anything.
  4. method ¶4 Set and record the seed; an unreproducible backtest is not evidence.
  5. caveat ¶5 Report the spread, not the best draw; best-of-twenty is chapter 25's selection bias applied to solver runs.
  6. method ¶6 Seed the initial population with the convex answer; a global hunt becomes local refinement; not the package default.

Note ¶2 repeats the parent's ¶3 (Gilli, repeated runs, the spread); no code, so the seed dependence is asserted, not measured, and goes to exercises 3 and 4; ¶3 is a one-sentence transition.

14 Risk-Based Allocation

3211 words · 5 sections · 33 paragraphs

Claim
Allocating risk rather than capital is a convex problem that a six-line fixed point solves to machine precision and a one-line inverse-volatility rule nearly solves, which the package's own risk-budget objective scores correctly and cannot find; what it buys is several times the effective bets for a few percent more volatility, on a covariance matrix that halves those bets in the credit crisis.
Question
Why how much money sits in a position is not how much risk sits there, and what allocating risk instead looks like; @qian2006 and @maillard2010, in the third sentence rather than the first two.
Why here
Stated once, in the first sentence: every portfolio so far was described by its capital, so the chapter follows the capital-allocating optimizers. Needs chapter 9's minimum-variance portfolio and its resampling, chapter 13's convex-versus-heuristic classification and its constraint-ignoring solver, chapter 7's rank bound and regime spectrum; hands hierarchical risk parity to chapter 15 and the estimation problem to chapter 19, and reads chapters 23 and 25 ahead for turnover and the out-of-sample register. Why it closes the part after the solvers is not stated beyond that classification.
Does
Decompose any portfolio's volatility into risk contributions, compute the equal-risk-contribution portfolio by fixed point or by inverse volatility, score a portfolio against the package's objective, use max_prisk for a cap, and audit concentration with HHI(), diversification() and an effective-bets count.
Order
Theory (Euler decomposition, equal risk contribution and its convex program, the inverse-volatility approximation), implementation (the package objective scored, five failed searches, the cap it can enforce, the concentration audit), application (the trade in bets and volatility with the out-of-sample line, the regime dependence of Σ, general budgets, hierarchical risk parity), eight takeaways, four exercises; deviations: the theory section already runs the package (inverse.volatility.weight(), an inline minimum-variance optimization) and defines the base spec, the implementation section is the chapter's negative finding rather than its method, and the application closes with two code-free theory subsections, one of which says it belongs in chapter 15.

Reader First alpha reading, 2026-11-13. Tie the chapter back to the global minimum-variance portfolio and to the other strategies of the *Lost in Translation* paper (minimum correlation, inverse volatility and the rest of its roster). Glad that hierarchical risk parity is in the book.

Allocating Risk Rather Than Capital 714 words

Says
Volatility decomposes exactly into risk contributions; equalizing them is a convex problem a fixed point solves in six lines and inverse volatility nearly solves in one.

The decomposition that makes this precise 180 words

Says
By Euler's theorem portfolio volatility is exactly the sum of each asset's risk contribution, and on sixteen equal weights one asset carries a multiple of its share.
Why here
stated: the exactness is what makes risk budgeting possible at all; ¶3's gap "is the entire motivation for what follows".
  1. derivation ¶1 Volatility is homogeneous of degree one, so Euler's theorem gives additive contributions (eq-14-euler).
  2. claim ¶2 The shares sum to the total by construction, @qian2006's point; divided by σ they sum to one, which the code computes.
  3. code risk-contribution
  4. result ¶3 Equal weights: the largest series carries a multiple of its one-sixteenth of the capital; the gap is the whole motivation.

Equal risk contribution 335 words

Says
The equal-risk-contribution portfolio exists, is unique long-only and is convex by @spinu2013; a six-line fixed point reaches it to machine precision and still gives the least volatile series the largest weight.
Why here
needs eq-14-euler to set its terms equal; stated as what the gap of ¶3 motivates; ¶2 and ¶4 point forward to the search.
  1. definition ¶1 Every term of eq-14-euler set equal (eq-14-erc).
  2. claim ¶2 @maillard2010: exists, unique long-only, volatility between minimum variance and equal weight; no closed form but not hard; @spinu2013's convex log-barrier program (eq-14-spinu), @bai2016; the package uses a metaheuristic on it.
  3. method ¶3 A fixed-point iteration converges in milliseconds.
  4. code erc
  5. result ¶4 Six lines reach equal contributions to machine precision; remember it when the search spends twenty thousand evaluations.
  6. code erc-weights
  7. code erc-claims
  8. result ¶5 US_government still gets the largest weight, being least volatile; far from the share minimum variance gives it, computed inline.

Note ¶2 and ¶4 both send the reader to the application section for the metaheuristic search, which sits in the implementation section; ¶5 runs a full minimum-variance optimization inside an inline expression, and the audit chunk computes the same portfolio again as w_mv.

The covariance is a regime variable 274 words

Says
The effective number of bets belongs to Σ, not to the portfolio, and the matrix's independent directions fall by more than half through the credit crisis.
Why here

  1. claim ¶1 The number of effective bets is a property of Σ, not of the portfolio, and Σ moves.
  2. code regime-spectrum
  3. result ¶2 Outside the episodes the first component carries less and the matrix spans more directions; through the credit crisis the count more than halves, so sixteen equal contributions become a handful of bets when it matters.
  4. caveat ¶3 Risk parity measures risk more honestly and then allocates against an estimate that halves when tested; @roncalli2013; chapter 19 confronts it.

Budgets that are not equal 177 words

Hierarchical risk parity 202 words

Says
Hierarchical risk parity clusters assets on correlation distance and allocates by recursive bisection without inverting Σ; it belongs with chapter 15 and is the one technique in this area the package lacks.
Why here
stated: the obvious next question once risk contributions are on the table, and the one widely used technique the package does not implement; the text says it belongs in chapter 15.
  1. definition ¶1 The methods here need Σ and mostly its inverse, weakest by chapter 7; @lopezdeprado2016 clusters, orders and bisects recursively instead.
  2. claim ¶2 No inversion, so defined even when Σ is singular (eq-07-rank); claimed more stable out of sample because a clustering is coarser than an inverse.
  3. transition ¶3 Belongs with the sleeve structure of chapter 15; mentioned here as the next question and as the package's gap.

Note no code, and the text itself says the method belongs elsewhere; ¶1's premise that most of the chapter's methods need the inverse sits against a fixed point and a one-liner that invert nothing.

Key Takeaways 253 words

Says
Eight bullets: the exact decomposition, equal capital is not equal risk, the equal-risk portfolio's properties, its convexity, the failed searches, the rule for the package objective, the trade and the out-of-sample line, bets as a property of Σ.
Why here
by contract.
  1. takeaway Volatility decomposes exactly by eq-14-euler; the shares sum by construction [@qian2006].
  2. takeaway Equal capital is not equal risk: one of sixteen equal weights carries a multiple of its share.
  3. takeaway The equal-risk portfolio exists, is unique long-only, and sits between minimum variance and equal weight in volatility [@maillard2010].
  4. takeaway Not a hard problem: eq-14-spinu is convex and six lines reach machine precision; the package routes it to a metaheuristic.
  5. takeaway Most of five solvers return their start, a tenfold budget changes nothing, and the one-liner beats a twenty-thousand-evaluation search.
  6. takeaway Use the risk-budget objective for caps and budgets, satisfiable by rejection; compute equal risk directly.
  7. takeaway Several times the effective bets for a few percent more volatility; out of sample inverse volatility sits between equal weight and tangency at a fraction of the turnover.
  8. takeaway Effective bets is a property of Σ, not of the portfolio, and falls by more than half into the credit crisis.

Note eight against "around five"; bullet 2 restates the decomposition's ¶3 and bullet 5 the search's ¶1 and ¶3, numbers included; the general budget and hierarchical risk parity have no bullet beyond bullet 6's rule.

  1. exercise 1. Run erc() on a covariance from the months outside the episodes, apply it through the credit crisis; how equal are the realized contributions?
  2. exercise 2. Build a universe where ignoring correlations is catastrophic, two near-identical series and one independent; report what inverse.volatility.weight() does.
  3. exercise 3. Repeat tbl-14-searches with max_prisk instead of min_concentration; do the solvers agree, does the budget bind?
  4. exercise 4. Replace entropy with the inverse Herfindahl of the risk shares and recompute tbl-14-concentration; which ranks change, which measure to defend to a client.

Note four against "around three"; each follows one subsection: 1 the regime, 2 the approximation, 3 the searches, 4 the audit table.

15 Hierarchical and Sleeve-Based Allocation

6717 words · 5 sections · 49 paragraphs

Claim
The two-layer organization (classes first, then selection inside a sleeve) is a governance decision whose cost can be measured: in sample the nested portfolio can never beat the flat one, and on sixteen classes it loses out of sample as well, at every window, because the cross-sleeve covariances stage 1 discards are information at this dimension; the case for the hierarchy arrives only where the flat matrix cannot be estimated.
Question
How should an allocation be organized when nobody can hold the whole problem in one head, and what does the organization cost; cites @sharpe1981 for the decentralized investment problem and @brinson1986 for how much the top-level decision explains.
Why here
Stated: this is the chapter in which the book's own structure (Parts III to V across the sixteen classes, Part VI inside a sleeve) is made explicit and priced, as a strategic decision in ch. 3's sense. Takes the flat problem, eq-09-gmv and eq-09-tangency from ch. 9, the shrinkage form and the rank finding from ch. 7, the hedging finding from ch. 4, the out-of-sample claim from ch. 8, the budget constraint from ch. 12, quadprog behind ROI from ch. 13, the missing HRP from ch. 14, the back-fill from ch. 23, the rolling backtest and the register from ch. 25. Hands the benchmarks to ch. 21 and ch. 22, the hedging instruction to ch. 22, the judging of the manager and the anchored variant to ch. 24. Why it opens Part V rather than closing Part IV is not stated.
Does
Partition the sixteen classes into sleeves, solve the nested problem by hand and with mult.portfolio.spec(), reproduce the package's proxy series, race nested against flat on a rolling window and check it against the register, and read a mixed-objective nested policy as a strategic asset allocation with benchmarks for the sleeve managers.
Order
Opening; theory (the two-stage problem, its shrinkage reading with two worked boxes, the benchmark reading, hedging); implementation (the one-asset failure, the package's method, the by-hand reproduction, the opt.list machinery); application (in sample, out of sample, the policy view); takeaways; exercises. Deviations: the theory section executes the chapter's solver, reads the in-sample ladder with its numbers inside a collapsed box, carries a 495-word box on a different method and a results table on hedging, and announces the out-of-sample verdict before the race runs; the two main headings have no paragraph of their own; the implementation's last subsection draws two figures no paragraph reads; no picture opens the application; six takeaways and four exercises.

Sleeves, and what is decided at each level 451 words

Says
The nested problem solves each sleeve on its own block and then allocates across the sleeve returns; the unrolled result is feasible for the flat problem, so in sample it can never have lower variance, and the gap is the price of decentralization.
Why here

  1. definition ¶1 Flat is Markowitz over all N; stage 1 solves each block, stage 2 allocates across sleeve returns; eq-15-stage1, eq-15-stage2.
  2. definition ¶2 The resulting weight is sleeve budget times within-sleeve weight; eq-15-unroll.
  3. claim ¶3 Objectives may differ by stage; what matters is that stage 1 sees one sleeve and stage 2 only K series.

The shrinkage reading — 1577 (728 before the two boxes)

Says
Stage 1 is the flat problem on the block-diagonal matrix, a shrinkage estimator whose intensity the mandate sets rather than the data; stage 2 restores the cross-sleeve information in K-by-K form; with a diagonal Ω the whole construction collapses to flat minimum variance on Σ_B, so flat, nested and block-diagonal form a ladder, and where nesting sits on it depends on whether the cross-block covariance is signal.
Why here
Stated in the opening: the problem is read as a shrinkage of the covariance matrix before it is implemented; needs eq-07-shrinkage and Ledoit–Wolf from ch. 7; the application reads its ladder and its verdict through this subsection.
  1. claim ¶1 Stage 1 is flat on Σ_B (eq-15-block): eq-07-shrinkage with intensity one off-block, set by the mandate; Stein, Michaud.
  2. derivation ¶2 Stage 2 restores K(K−1)/2 covariances; diagonal Ω collapses to flat on Σ_B (eq-15-identity); the three-rung ladder.
  3. code cross-block S, the same-sleeve mask, S_block; cross-block share of the squared mass, mean within and cross correlation
  4. result ¶3 Cross-sleeve entries carry a share of the squared mass; the race decides, and its answer follows Jagannathan–Ma.
  5. method ¶4 All of it is ch. 9's long-only QP, solved over a thousand times; quadprog called directly, checked once against ROI.
  6. code gmv-qp gmv_qp(), gmv_spec(); w_flat from ROI asserted equal; mu, p_vol(), p_ret(), p_sr(), eff_bets(); the flat weights
  7. transition ¶5 (callout, collapsed) Lead-in: stage 1, stage 2 with diagonal Ω, and the unroll.
  8. code identity-check within_gmv() with the one-asset case, unroll() asserting name order; inverse-variance budgets equal flat on S_block
  9. result callout ¶6 Inverse-variance budgets and flat on Σ_B agree to machine precision under long-only; nested proper uses the full Ω.
  10. code nested-in-sample sleeve_returns(), Omega, b_nest, w_nested; the one negative sleeve correlation asserted
  11. result callout ¶7 Sleeve correlations: government–credit positive, equity–government the one negative; stage 2 can act on it, stage 1 could not.

Hedging is a sleeve decision 378 words

16 Signals and Return Forecasts

7301 words · 5 sections · 50 paragraphs

Claim
Of four signals on sixteen series only the theory-motivated term spread forecasts out of sample, and a restrained tilt on it pays a few tens of basis points of certainty equivalent after costs, inside the Sharpe ratio's sampling error; the signals that data alone motivate, and a regularized seven-signal model, lose to the historical mean and pay more to trade.
Question
Can the excess return on an asset class be forecast, and does a forecast that fits the past help an investor who must act before the future arrives; cites @welch2008 (hardly any predictor helped in real time) and @campbell2008 (a small but meaningful gain under a sign restriction), who between them fixed the two criteria: the out-of-sample R² against the mean and the investor's certainty-equivalent gain.
Why here

Testing one forecast against another 198 words

Says
The Diebold–Mariano test on the loss differential is biased against a nested model, and the Clark–West correction is the statistic reported, with DM beside it.
Why here

A tilt around the policy 701 words

  5. result ¶3 The average move against the band and the share where the band binds; modest forecasts, modest tilt.

17 Expressing Views

7975 words · 5 sections · 65 paragraphs

Claim
A view should enter the optimizer blended with the equilibrium prior at a stated confidence, which makes the portfolio the market plus a position in each view portfolio; entropy pooling buys the same on a skewed history at a price in effective months, a ranking supplies a direction whose size the allocator must still choose, and any view written into μ without the covariance becomes a hedged spread trade.
Question
How does an allocator's opinion become a portfolio weight; cites Black and Litterman (1992) in the second sentence, with Michaud (1989) for why it must not become one directly.
Why here
Names itself tactical in chapter 3's sense, a deviation from the strategic policy chapter 6 anchored on the equilibrium; takes the data from chapter 2 and the excess basis from chapter 3, μ and Σ and the GMV and effective-bets ideas from chapter 9, the equilibrium prior (rebuilt verbatim), tangency_lo(), make_moments(), the match.call() trap and the Σ_μ warning from chapter 6, skewness from chapter 4, the long-only QP from chapter 12, standard errors from chapters 6 and 8; hands forward to chapters 16 and 25 (data-generated views and their backtest), 18 (skipping μ), 19 (a regime as a scenario view through eq-17-entropy) and 28 (confidence as the size of an uncertainty set). Why it follows chapter 16 inside the part: not stated; the closing paragraph relates the two (a signal has a history of views, an opinion does not) without saying why the opinion comes second.
Does
Write the Black–Litterman posterior with an explicit confidence and check it against black.litterman(); route views through set.portfolio.moments() with the covariance restored; express mean, volatility and ranking views as entropy constraints and price them in effective months; turn a ranking into a centroid, name and scale it, and tell a coherent view from a hedged spread.
Order
Theory (Black–Litterman posterior, entropy pooling, centroid), implementation (prior rebuilt, Black–Litterman with two views, the package route, entropy pooling, ordinal views), application (one opinion three ways), takeaways, exercises. Deviations: the implementation holds 4,551 of 7,975 words, three of the four figures and three of the four labeled tables, and reads results in every subsection, so the application is a fourth worked case rather than the results section; a theory property (a centroid alone is a corner solution at any scale) is argued in the implementation; the chapter's coherence condition (the shift in μ must be Σ times a portfolio) is stated only in the application; four exercises.

A View as a Bayesian Update 1268 words

Says
Three formulations of a view: a normal update of the equilibrium mean whose confidence sets the position, a minimum-divergence reweighting of historical scenarios priced in effective months, and a ranking whose centroid is a direction without level or scale.

The Black-Litterman posterior 628 words

Says
With a normal prior on the equilibrium mean and Meucci's Ω, τ cancels, each view lands at a confidence-weighted average of prior and view, and the posterior portfolio is the market plus one position per view portfolio.
Why here
"The standard answer" of the opening; needs chapter 9's μ and Σ, eq-06-equilibrium and Sharpe's reverse optimization; the entropy subsection opens by removing its two restrictions, the implementation traces the confidence it ends on, and the application shows what happens without eq-17-weights.
  1. definition ¶1 μ and Σ as in chapter 9; the prior on μ is normal, centered on chapter 6's equilibrium vector; eq-17-prior.
  2. definition ¶2 τ scales the prior's uncertainty; a view is K linear statements on μ with error covariance Ω; eq-17-views.
  3. derivation ¶3 Absolute and relative rows of P; normal prior and likelihood give a normal posterior; eq-17-posterior mean and covariance.
  4. claim ¶4 Derivation cited to four papers; two features: the update moves only along ΣP', and Σ_μ is never a risk input.
  5. definition ¶5 τ and Ω have no data behind them; τ conventions surveyed, τ cancels; Ω conventions surveyed, Meucci's closed form eq-17-omega.
  6. derivation ¶6 Substituting eq-17-omega, τ cancels and each view portfolio's posterior is a weighted average of prior and view; eq-17-pooling.
  7. claim ¶7 c = ½ weighs both equally, 1 imposes the view, 0 ignores it; the confidence is the position's size.
  8. derivation ¶8 He–Litterman's result: with the market's risk aversion the posterior weights are w_mkt + P'Λ; eq-17-weights.
  9. claim ¶9 Market plus one position per view portfolio; unnamed assets keep their weight, unlike under a hand-written μ; long-only clips.

Note ¶4 does three jobs (the derivation's literature, the ΣP' property, the Σ_μ warning carried from chapter 6); ¶9's closing clause, that eq-17-weights holds only until a weight reaches zero, is what the implementation's ¶8 and the application's ¶4 both rest on.

Views on scenarios: entropy pooling 400 words

Says
A view is a constraint on the probabilities of the sample months, the posterior is the least-divergent probability vector that satisfies it, and the effective number of scenarios prices what the view cost.
Why here
"Black-Litterman is a view on the mean under normality, and Meucci removes both restrictions": placed as the generalization, and it needs Black–Litterman stated to say it reproduces it on normal scenarios; hands eq-17-entropy to chapter 19 and eq-17-cost to the implementation's cost table.
  1. definition ¶1 Meucci drops normality and the mean-only restriction: the prior is T uniform scenarios, a view any constraint on expectations; eq-17-entropy.
  2. evidence ¶2 Rows of H and F are view functions; Kullback, Jaynes, Csiszár, Robertson cited; the solution is an exponential tilt, eq-17-tilt.
  3. claim ¶3 One multiplier per view; the tilt reproduces Black–Litterman on normal scenarios and parts from it otherwise, measured in the application.
  4. definition ¶4 A concentrated posterior has thrown history away; the diagnostic is the effective number of scenarios, eq-17-effective.
  5. derivation ¶5 Chapter 9's effective-bets construction; T under the uniform prior, falling toward one; a k-standard-error view costs k²/2T; eq-17-cost.
  6. claim ¶6 Near views cost almost nothing, far ones leave a fraction of the history; opinion pooling of probabilities gives eq-17-pooling's form.

Note ¶5 derives eq-17-cost in one clause ("to second order") with no pointer to appendix B; ¶2 is a four-citation genealogy standing between the problem and its solution.

18 Parametric Portfolio Policies

6589 words · 5 sections · 44 paragraphs

Claim
Writing each weight as the benchmark weight plus a linear tilt in standardized characteristics, and estimating the few coefficients on realized utility, replaces the moments whose estimation error chapter 8 priced; on the modeled market both policies finish ahead of their benchmarks out of sample, by margins the sample cannot certify, and what pays is the one characteristic whose coefficient stayed stable.
Question

Why here

Hands the long-only equity policy and the value characteristic to chapter 21 and the many-parameter generalization to chapter 27. Why it follows 17 and precedes 19 inside the part is not stated.
Does

The policy and its estimator 476 words

Says
The policy is the benchmark weight plus a tilt linear in standardized characteristics, its coefficients maximize realized power utility without a mean or a covariance, and because the portfolio return is linear in θ the policy is a position in K + 1 portfolios.
Why here

  2. claim ¶2 One θ for all assets and months; tilts sum to zero; 1/N_t caps the active position; θ = 0 is the benchmark.
  3. definition ¶3 eq-18-estimator: average power utility, whole distribution, no μ̂ or Σ̂; Brandt, Aït-Sahalia and Brandt, Brandt's survey.

19 Regime-Dependent Allocation

7597 words · 5 sections · 47 paragraphs

Claim
Switching to a stress portfolio beats the static policy only when the regimes are dated with hindsight; detected in real time by a turbulence threshold or a filtered two-state model the switch keeps little or none of that advantage, neither detector is distinguishable from the static policy by a paired test, and holding the stress portfolio throughout has a higher Sharpe ratio than either, so on this market whether to hold minimum variance matters more than when.
Question
What if the right portfolio depends on the state of the world; @hamilton1989 for the statistical form and @ang2002a for carrying it into portfolio choice, both inside the first two sentences.
Why here
Stated in the opening: the descriptive chapters supplied the reason to ask, chapter 4's episodes of rising correlations and chapter 14's falling effective bets, and the chapter is tactical in chapter 3's sense. Needs chapter 5's turbulence index, chapter 9's tangency portfolio, chapter 8's quadratic program, chapter 16's lag-and-assert discipline, chapter 23's turnover and cost accounting and chapter 25's register; hands the wrong-model question to chapter 28 and the design-leak problem to chapter 25. Why it follows the parametric policies of chapter 18, or sits where it does in this part, is not stated.
Does
Write a Hamilton filter, a Kim smoother and EM for a two-state model, build a turbulence flag and real-time regime probabilities with no look-ahead, run regime.portfolios() knowing its seven behaviors, and test a switching strategy against the static policy and against its own constant mix with a paired Sharpe test.
Order
Theory (model, filter and smoother, mixture moments, two rules, dating against detecting, turbulence), implementation (regimes dated with hindsight, the turbulence threshold, the hidden Markov model with its real-time refit and detection table, the package's regime machinery and its quirks), application (six strategies under the register's accounting, the reading, the episode table, three limits), five takeaways, four exercises; deviations: the chapter's central result, the detection figure and table the text calls "the chapter's argument in small", lands inside the implementation section with the package subsection between it and the application, and the application opens with a method subsection whose figure is never read.

Regimes, Filters, and Mixtures 1213 words

Says
A regime-switching model is a Markov chain plus a filter; the investor faces mixture moments with a between-regime term, uses the state by mixing or by switching, and must detect the regime rather than date it, with turbulence as the parameter-free detector.
Why here
by contract; the opening's plan names the model, the filter and the mixture moments first. Paragraphs: none of its own; the section goes straight to its first subsection.

The regime-switching model 120 words

Says
Returns are Gaussian within a state that follows a Markov chain whose transition matrix fixes regime durations and long-run shares.
Why here
not stated; the definitions the filter needs.
  1. definition ¶1 @hamilton1989's model (eq-19-model); expected duration and long-run share from P; @turner1989's two-state version, @ang2012's survey.

The Hamilton filter 224 words

Says
The state is inferred by a prediction and an update step whose normalizer is the likelihood; EM maximizes it with smoothed probabilities, and the filtered–smoothed difference is the chapter's subject.
Why here
stated: the state is never observed, so everything rests on its probability given the returns seen so far.
  1. derivation ¶1 Notation ξ and η; prediction (eq-19-predict) and update (eq-19-update); log-likelihood from the denominator; EM by @dempster1977, @baum1970, @hamilton1990.
  2. definition ¶2 EM needs smoothed probabilities, @kim1994's backward recursion (eq-19-smooth); filtered against smoothed is the chapter's subject.

Note ¶2's last sentence states the chapter's thesis inside a definition.

One-step-ahead moments as a mixture 236 words

Says
Next month's return is a mixture whose covariance carries a between-regime term and whose tails are fat and asymmetric from Gaussian ingredients, jointly across assets.
Why here
stated: the investor at t does not know s_{t+1}, so faces the predicted probabilities' mixture; the between-regime term and the kurtosis are reused by the worked example.
  1. derivation ¶1 With π the predicted probabilities, mixture mean and covariance (eq-19-mixture).
  2. claim ¶2 Between-regime term; non-normal mixture, kurtosis (eq-19-kurtosis), skewness [@timmermann2000]; chapter 4's tails from Gaussian parts; joint switching, chapter 11, @ang2002.

Note ¶2 does four things: interprets the between-regime term, derives the kurtosis, links to chapter 4's stylized facts, and links to chapter 11's co-moments and the correlation-asymmetry literature.

Two ways to use the state 261 words

Says
Given the state probabilities an investor either holds the tangency portfolio of the mixture moments or switches between regime portfolios on the most probable state, and the literature finds both worth something against a static policy.
Why here
stated: "given π there are two rules"; the switching rule (eq-19-switch) is what the package implements and the application tests.
  1. definition ¶1 Mixture rule: tangency of eq-09-tangency on eq-19-mixture, weights move continuously; switching rule: one of K portfolios on the argmax (eq-19-switch).
  2. evidence ¶2 Compromise against bet; @clarke1998, @ang2002a, @ang2004, @guidolin2007, @guidolin2008, @tu2010, @bulla2011, @nystrup2015 on costs of ignoring regimes and simple switches.
  3. transition ¶3 All condition on an estimated state, and the estimate is where the difficulty lies.

Note ¶2 carries eight citations in one paragraph; ¶3 is one sentence turning the section toward dating and detecting.

Dating and detecting 214 words

Says
Dating uses the whole sample and detecting only the past, so detection lags; a regime backtest leaks through smoothed probabilities, full-sample parameters and a regime definition settled after looking.
Why here
stated by ¶3 of the previous subsection, the estimate is the difficulty; the two coding leaks are "asserted away below" and the design leak is sent to chapter 25.
  1. definition ¶1 Dating (peaks and troughs [@pagan2003], named episodes, smoothed probabilities) against detecting (filtered, parameters through t); the lag [@chauvet2008].
  2. caveat ¶2 Three leaks: smoothed probabilities, full-sample parameters, the regime definition; two are chapter 16's coding errors, one chapter 25's design problem.

Turbulence as a model-free detector 152 words

Says
The turbulence index of chapter 5 has no parameters to estimate, is persistent and poorly rewarded while it lasts, and a quantile threshold on it is the simplest detector there is.
Why here
stated: a regime model has parameters to estimate before it says anything, so a parameter-free detector is set beside it.
  1. definition ¶1 Eq-05-turbulence needs only trailing moments; @chow1999's blend is eq-19-mixture's first sum; @kritzman2010, @kritzman2012; threshold at a quantile of its past.

Says

  5. takeaway Dated switch well ahead; turbulence keeps a small part, the filter none, neither distinguishable; stress throughout beats both; rates shock defeats every switch.

20 Sustainable Investing Constraints

7748 words · 5 sections · 40 paragraphs

Claim
A sustainability constraint that does not bind costs nothing; one that binds costs tracking error and return at the rate its multiplier prices, paid through the priced characteristics the rating is correlated with; it is a cost and never an alpha, so the chapter prices the constraint rather than the names.
Question
What does an exclusion or a rating floor cost a portfolio, and who pays? Cited to @pedersen2021 for the cost (the ESG-efficient frontier) and @pastor2021 for who pays (tastes priced in equilibrium).
Why here
Takes the multiplier and the constraint functions of ch. 12, the shrinkage of ch. 7, the policy of ch. 15 and the strategic/tactical vocabulary of ch. 3; builds on the equity sleeve, its capitalization anchoring and the policy recomputation of ch. 21, a later chapter, and uses the tracking-error and standard-error equations of ch. 24. Forward: ch. 25 (what a screen does to a backtest's error bar) and ch. 28 (stability across windows). Why it closes Allocation Strategies: not stated; the text places it only on the horizon axis, as strategic.
Does
Construct rating levels from a path of changes, impose an exclusion, a floor and a best-in-class floor on an equity sleeve, price each by active share, tracking error and multiplier, audit the solutions, and measure the cost out of sample and across raters.
Order
Theory (five subsections, one paragraph each) → implementation (levels, sleeve, exclusion, floor, multiplier check, audit) → application (out-of-sample costs, then a second rater) → takeaways → exercises. Contract kept; the real-data callout closes the implementation from inside "The audit", and the chapter's closing sentences sit inside the last application subsection.

Tastes, Floors, and Exclusions 1140 words

Says
A taste and a floor trace one frontier priced by the floor's multiplier; an exclusion is a box at zero with a Jagannathan–Ma price; a tilt is a factor exposure on a score; and the score is an estimate whose taste is priced in equilibrium.
Why here
The opening's plan puts the problem first; the two framing results of the opening are what the section works out, and every object the implementation builds (eq-20-floor, eq-20-exclusion, eq-20-bic) is defined here. Paragraphs: none at this level; the section goes straight to its first subsection.

Note Five subsections, each a single paragraph of 160 to 300 words; the theory of the chapter is five paragraphs.

The investor's problem with a taste for a rating 301 words

Says
A taste for a rating and a floor on it produce the same portfolio, the floor's multiplier being the taste, so a binding floor costs variance at the shadow price and a slack one costs nothing.
Why here
First because the opening's plan starts with the investor's problem; needs the investor of ch. 3 and the Lagrangian and shadow price of ch. 12; eq-20-floor is what the tilt subsection and the implementation impose.
  1. derivation ¶1 Taste term and ESG-efficient frontier; floor traces it, θ = λ_s; slackness prices it; convex QP; preference, not information.

Note One 300-word paragraph around two display equations makes five moves; the last, that a floor is a preference and not information about the estimates, is a claim of its own.

Exclusion as a restriction of the universe 304 words

An exclusion is a box at zero; for a capitalization-weighted sleeve its active share is the benchmark weight removed, and for an optimized sleeve a binding exclusion is an implicit risk view priced by its multiplier.
Why here
Called the oldest form of the constraint; order as in the opening's plan. Needs eq-12-jm and the tracking error of ch. 24 (later); the implementation's exclusion and exclusion-jm chunks check both readings.

Tilts as a factor-exposure constraint on a score 157 words

A floor is a factor-exposure constraint on the score, a best-in-class floor is one such row per group in centered scores, and the second costs more because it cannot buy the rating where it is cheapest.
Why here

21 The Equity Sleeve

6729 words · 5 sections · 33 paragraphs

Claim
Selection survives the allocation only in the sense the arithmetic allows: the sleeve's information ratio reaches the total intact while its active return and tracking error arrive scaled by the equity budget ω, and on this market what the total registers is the selected sleeve's lower variance, not an alpha that the out-of-sample months cannot distinguish from zero.
Question
Which stocks should fill an equity sleeve whose budget and benchmark are fixed from above, and is whatever the selection earns still visible once the sleeve is one line of the allocation; @fama1992 and @fama1993 for selection by sorting as a method, @treynor1973 for combining the active sleeve with the passive portfolio.
Why here
Stated: the first chapter to work inside a class rather than across classes, the granularity axis of chapter 3 made concrete on the 123 equities behind the five series that Parts III to V allocated as single numbers. Takes from chapter 2 the capitalization-weighted indices and the independence of the idiosyncratic draws, from chapter 5 the lagged proxy and the within-region lesson, from chapter 7 the rank bound, the repairs and the race, from chapter 12 the dropped position limit, from chapter 15 the policy and the sub-portfolio mechanics, from chapter 18 the parametric estimate of the same characteristics; sends forward to chapter 22 (a duration in place of a characteristic), chapter 23 (the cost of the trading) and chapter 25 (the register). Its mandate, active-weight notation, fundamental law, information-ratio standard error, budget figure and cone solver are all taken from chapter 24, three chapters later, which the text treats throughout as already read.
Does
Rebuild a capitalization-weighted benchmark from its instruments with the right lag, run within-region characteristic sorts with Newey–West inference, build a factor or shrunk covariance on a universe wider than the window, solve a minimum-variance sleeve under a tracking-error budget by a one-parameter quadratic family and a root search, wrap sleeves as sub-portfolios, and trace a sleeve's active return into the total portfolio.
Order
Theory (mandate and the ω arithmetic, sorts, equal weighting, covariance with the cardinality limit and the tracking-error family), implementation (benchmark rebuilt, equal against cap, sorts, covariance race, the budgeted sleeve, the sub-portfolio), application in one undivided section (policy recomputed, three fillers, sleeve rows, total rows, sampling error, the figure), five takeaways, four exercises; deviations: the application opens with code and a table and reaches its only figure last, and the sub-portfolio subsection builds a structure the application then sets aside.

The covariance matrix of a sleeve 401 words

Says
With 123 names any window under 124 months is singular, so the sleeve needs shrinkage or a k-factor structure; a cardinality limit is a mixed-integer problem whose convex substitute is a cap; and the budget on a minimum-variance sleeve is a one-parameter blend of benchmark and minimum variance found by a root search.
Why here
stated: everything the sleeve optimizes needs Σ, and chapter 7's rank bound says the sample estimate cannot deliver it on this universe.
  1. definition ¶1 eq-07-rank: singular under 124 months; shrinkage (@ledoit2003, @ledoit2004) or BB' + D (eq-21-factor-cov); @connor1993, @bai2002; settled out of sample.
  2. derivation ¶2 Within region estimable but thin; cardinality is MIP (@bienstock1996), a cap its substitute (@jagannathan2003); the budget's Lagrangian gives v(θ) (eq-21-family); @roll1992.

Note ¶2 holds three topics, the within-region parameter count, the cardinality limit and the tracking-error family; the family is the chapter's own device and arrives at the end of a subsection named for the covariance matrix.

22 The Fixed Income Sleeve

7199 words · 5 sections · 52 paragraphs

Claim
Duration targeting and portfolio optimization are one problem: a bond portfolio is a set of exposures to level, slope, curvature and spread, so a duration target is a factor_exposure bound on a minimum-variance program, its level strategic and its term-spread overlay tactical, while abroad the currency, not the duration, carries most of the risk.
Question
What changes in portfolio construction when the asset has a duration; cites @litterman1991 (three curve factors explain nearly all variation in government bond returns, so exposures describe a portfolio better than its list of bonds), with @nelson1987 and @diebold2006 for the form of the curve.
Why here
Stated: the sleeve is the bond part of the allocation, one level below the class series on which ch. 15 allocates, and on ch. 3's horizon axis its duration is strategic and its curve positioning tactical. Takes the data and the hedge identity from ch. 2 and appendix A, the currency share from ch. 4, the bond block's rank deficiency from ch. 7, the home-bond argument, the naming rule and the full_investment degeneracy from ch. 9, the quadratic program from ch. 12 and ch. 13, the units, the month-end read and the surviving term spread from ch. 16, the policy and its estimator from ch. 18; it uses ahead of their chapters the per-class costs of ch. 23 and the register's start and backtest of ch. 25, and points to ch. 10 for a skewness it does not compute. Hands the cost of maintaining the exposures to ch. 23, whether one sample is evidence to ch. 25, the ignored exposure bounds to ch. 28 and the loadings to the scenario appendix. Why it follows ch. 21 is not stated: the equity sleeve, its only sibling in the part, is never mentioned.
Does
Split a bond return into carry, rolldown, duration and convexity; read durations and maturities from factor regressions; recognize the shipped factors as one rotation of three yields beside Nelson–Siegel loadings and principal components; build a minimum-variance sleeve under duration and slope bounds while guarding three silent package behaviors; fit and bootstrap a term-spread overlay; backtest sleeves against the class ladder and read them by episode.
Order
Opening (question and claim, roadmap with the strategic and tactical labels, data, one remark on the data chunk); theory in six subsections (return decomposition, three factors, credit, duration target as constraint, currency, predictability); implementation in five (units, exposures and rotations with a worked box, currency and the fit, the constrained sleeve with a warning callout, the overlay); application as one unbroken section (four sleeves defined, backtests, a callout, three tables read in turn, a closing paragraph); takeaways; exercises. Deviations: the theory runs a chunk and reads its result (the currency share) and carries inline sample numbers on credit; the theory orders credit, target, currency, predictability where the implementation orders exposures, currency, sleeve, overlay; the overlay's in-sample fit, a result, sits in the implementation; the credit sleeve is specified only in the application's opening paragraph and its backtest chunk; every sleeve is unhedged although theory and implementation both argue that an unhedged duration target controls a minority of the risk, and the hedged sleeve is exercise 1; the application opens with a method paragraph and four chunks, not a picture, and has no subsections; fig-22-factors is never read; five takeaways, four exercises.

Says

  3. derivation ¶3 K factors drive yields; eq-22-exposure; equal exposures mean equal risk; K = 3 is Litterman–Scheinkman; hence a sleeve of exposures.

23 Rebalancing and Transaction Costs

3281 words · 5 sections · 34 paragraphs

Claim
Free monthly rebalancing is worth different amounts to different strategies: under per-class linear costs the Sharpe ratio lost to trading is ordered by how much a strategy optimizes, rebalancing less often helps the two rule-based portfolios at every estimation window and moves the three optimized ones by more, in a direction that changes with the window, and theory's answer is a no-trade region or a partial adjustment rather than a return to target.
Question
What the assumption of free monthly rebalancing to target is worth, and to which strategies; posed as a statement about the book so far, not as a question, with @peroldshortfall1988 (the implementation shortfall) in the third sentence rather than the first two.
Why here
Stated in the first sentence only: every portfolio so far was rebalanced monthly at no charge. Draws on chapter 4 (the Sharpe ranks of tbl-04-moments, the wandering rolling mean, the credit crisis), chapter 9 (the optimizers, and maximum Sharpe as that chapter's best portfolio), chapter 14 (inverse volatility) and chapter 18 (costs in the objective by construction); reads every measurement from the out-of-sample runs it attributes to chapter 25, two chapters ahead, said to have been computed with and without costs for this purpose; hands contribution to chapter 24 and the sources of the cost rates to appendix 93. Why it opens the part, ahead of the backtesting chapter whose runs it reads, is not stated.
Does

Order

Turnover 305 words

What theory says about how often to trade 191 words

Says
With costs in the objective the optimal policy is a no-trade region around the target or, for a moving target, a partial adjustment toward an aim portfolio at a rate that falls as costs rise.
Why here
the plan's second step; ¶2 says its distinction (a fast target trades partially and often, a stable one drifts) is what the measurements below recover.
  1. definition ¶1 @constantinides1986 and @davis1990: a no-trade region; inside it do nothing, outside it trade back to the boundary only.
  2. derivation ¶2 @garleanu2013: partial adjustment toward a cost-weighted aim portfolio (eq-23-partial); fast targets trade partially and often, stable ones drift.

Note ¶2 promises that the measurements recover the distinction; the application then reports the optimizers gaining from annual rebalancing at two of three windows and reads the equation as explaining the size of the move, not its sign; heading is a clause.

Calendar rules against band rules 259 words

Says
A band policy trades when a weight leaves its tolerance rather than on a date; the two families fail in opposite ways, and the literature finds the width matters far more than the trigger.
Why here
stated in ¶1: everything measured above rebalances on a calendar, which eq-23-partial suggests is not the natural policy; picks up the no-trade region of the theory and the EOP.Weight history of the implementation.
  1. definition ¶1 Everything above uses a calendar; a band policy trades when a weight leaves its tolerance, the no-trade region in practice.
  2. claim ¶2 Opposite failures: a calendar ignores what happened; a band has unpredictable turnover and can trade repeatedly across a boundary.
  3. evidence ¶3 @tokat2007: family matters less than width; @donohue2003 recover the no-trade region by dynamic programming; the width is the decision.
  4. caveat ¶4 The package has no band policy; a few lines around EOP.Weight would compare drifted weights to target each month.

Note prose only inside the application: no band rule is run on the modeled market, and ¶4 describes the code in words instead of showing it; the material is theory and literature, continuing "What theory says about how often to trade".

Key Takeaways 294 words

Says
Seven bullets: the two linear definitions, per-class costs, the no-trade and partial-adjustment policies, the frequency result, the monotone bite, the two-backend constraint, the back-fill.
Why here
by contract.
  1. takeaway Turnover is eq-23-turnover and its cost eq-23-net; both linear, defensible at asset-class scale, not for a single security.
  2. takeaway Costs are per class, government against credit in basis points; the dearest class carries a top-three Sharpe ratio.
  3. takeaway With costs, trade back only to the edge of a no-trade region, or adjust partially toward a moving aim (eq-23-partial).
  4. takeaway Rarer rebalancing helps the rules at every window; for the optimizers the sign depends on the window; the sixty-month numbers.

  6. takeaway A turnover constraint is hard under ROI and a penalty under CVXR; proportional costs exist only under ROI.
  7. takeaway Return.portfolio() back-fills equal weights before the first weight vector and only warns; a rolling window always creates that gap.

24 Benchmarks and Active Risk

7244 words · 5 sections · 52 paragraphs

Claim
Whether a portfolio beat its benchmark depends on the benchmark chosen and on what the mandate promised, and whether that was skill cannot be settled at this sample length: against one stated policy the book's minimum-variance manager has a negative information ratio, a positive alpha and a higher Sharpe ratio; a tracking-error budget alone buys dominated portfolios until total risk is bounded as well; the benchmark carries a bet of its own; and the information ratio's interval contains zero.
Question
Did the portfolio beat its benchmark, and if it did, was that skill; cites @grinold1989 for the modern form of the question (the fundamental law) and @grinold2000 for the vocabulary mandates are written in.
Why here
Stated only as a place on the horizon axis of ch. 3: the chapter is about evaluation rather than either horizon, and its one construction, the anchored allocation, is strategic because it tilts from ch. 15's policy. Takes the sixteen series and the hedged series from ch. 2, the dependence finding from ch. 4, the lagged capitalization proxy and the rolling betas from ch. 5, the estimation error from ch. 8, the Merton constants, eq-09-gmv, the frontier, dominance, the variance drag and the minimum-variance portfolio from ch. 9, group constraints from ch. 12, the four sleeves from ch. 15, the equal-weighting question from ch. 21, the back-fill and the turnover formula from ch. 23. Leans forward on ch. 25 five times as if already read: the register that ranks its manager, the rebalancing call, eq-25-brinson, the Ibbotson–Kaplan reminder, the intervals beside every Sharpe ratio. Names nothing a later chapter takes from it; why it stands between ch. 23 and ch. 25 is not stated.
Does
Measure a rebalanced portfolio against a policy with PerformanceAnalytics (tracking error, active premium, information ratio, capture ratios, alpha, beta, appraisal ratio, active share) on correctly aligned series; trace the tracking-error frontier in dual form with PortfolioAnalytics and under a budget and a total-risk bound with CVXR; attribute an active return by sleeve; put an interval on an information ratio.
Order
Opening; theory (the vocabulary and the fundamental law, the budget problem with Roll's solution and Jorion's repair, the Brinson decomposition, the standard error); implementation (the policy and the manager, the two return series, the measures, the package's active constraint, the CVXR budget, Jorion's bound); application (attribution by sleeve, two equity benchmarks, the error bar); takeaways; exercises. Deviations: the implementation is 47 percent of the chapter, reads its main results (the manager's three conflicting statistics, the tracking-error frontier, the anchored allocation) and holds its only figure; it turns halfway from measuring an out-of-sample manager to constructing an in-sample tilt without saying so; the implementation and application headings have no paragraph of their own; the application is three topics joined by a title and has no picture; the theory executes one chunk and reads it; six takeaways and four exercises.

  5. result ¶3 The interval contains zero and understates the truth; no evidence of skill either way; the test for a difference named.

25 Backtesting and Diagnostics

3366 words · 5 sections · 53 paragraphs

Claim
A backtest is a statistical estimate, not a replay of history: the package's rolling loop completes at full length while storing periods that never solved, those periods fall in the one stretch where the mandate was infeasible, and on the book's register every Sharpe-ratio interval overlaps every other, so what can be kept is what is not an estimate: turnover, cost and the failure count.
Question
Not posed as a question; implied: where the book's out-of-sample lines come from and how much of a backtest to believe; @harvey2015 and @lopezdeprado2018, in the sixth sentence rather than the first two.
Why here
Stated in the first sentence: every construction chapter ends with a line of out-of-sample evidence, and this is where those lines come from. Takes chapter 19's argument against an unchanged world, chapter 4's equity bear market and its non-i.i.d. finding, chapter 23's net figures and per-asset contributions, chapter 24's attribution, chapter 5's lagged market proxy and chapter 2's real-data sleeves; hands the cost of forecast error to chapter 26 and model doubt to chapter 28. Why it closes the part, after rebalancing and benchmarks, is not stated.
Does
Run a rolling backtest with windows of the intended length, find the periods that did not solve with diagnose_rebalancing(), carry and count them with pm_backtest(), register a parallel backend, put @lo2002's standard error on a Sharpe ratio, and name four kinds of look-ahead.

What a Backtest Estimates 264 words

Says
A backtest is a loop that may see only the past and its return series is a sample; two arguments of the package's loop decide how much past each step sees.

A backtest is a measurement, and it has an error bar 379 words

Says
With @lo2002's standard error every strategy's Sharpe-ratio interval overlaps every other, so no difference in the register is distinguishable from zero; turnover, cost and the failure count are not estimates and survive.
Why here
Stated in the opening's plan as the last step; needs reg and Tn from the register, and returns to the theory section's framing of eq-25-backtest as a sample.
  1. definition ¶1 The Sharpe ratios are point estimates; under i.i.d. returns @lo2002 gives the asymptotic standard error (eq-25-se), annualized below.
  2. code error-bar standard error and interval per strategy; asserts that all intervals overlap
  3. result ¶2 Every interval overlaps every other; no difference among the five, best against worst included, is distinguishable from zero.
  4. caveat ¶3 Chapter 4 rejects i.i.d., so intervals are wider (@ledoit2008; @jobson1981, @memmel2003); five strategies need little multiple-testing correction (@white2000, @sullivan1999, @bailey2014).
  5. claim ¶4 Two things survive: turnover and cost are arithmetic on observed trades, and the failure count is no estimate.
  6. claim ¶5 The conclusions worth keeping do not hang on a Sharpe ratio's third decimal; most useful ones do not.
  7. transition ¶6 Chapter 26 measures the cost of forecast error directly; chapter 28 asks what to do when the model is doubted.

Note ¶2 reads differences off overlapping single intervals, and ¶3 then names the test for a difference without running it; ¶4's failure count is the mandate's, a strategy outside the register; ¶6 is the chapter's send-off, with two subsections still to come.

A taxonomy of look-ahead 234 words

Says

Why here

  1. claim ¶1 eq-25-backtest lets the weights see only the past; violations are the commonest defect and rarely obvious; four kinds recur.
  2. caveat ¶2 A misaligned window uses data through t; chapter 5's one-period lag in the market proxy exists for this.
  3. caveat ¶3 A universe chosen with hindsight: every modeled asset survives to the sample's end, unlike chapter 2's real-data sleeves.
  4. caveat ¶4 Parameters tuned on the scoring sample make it in-sample [@harvey2015]; hence the sweep reports all nine configurations.
  5. caveat ¶5 Vendor data are revised; the current vintage of a macroeconomic series holds numbers nobody had then.
  6. claim ¶6 The first is a coding error, the rest design decisions; @lo2004: a same-history test measures the regime, not the rule.

Note Code-free theory as the chapter's last body subsection; "nine configurations" is the first mention of the sweep's size, of which the register subsection showed three windows; absent from the opening's plan, the takeaways and the exercises.

Key Takeaways 240 words

Says
Seven bullets: the backtest as an estimate, the inclusive window, the stored failure, the clustered failures, diagnosing and reporting the count, the unregistered backend, and the overlapping intervals against what is not an estimate.
Why here
Contract position.
  1. takeaway ¶1 A backtest is eq-25-backtest, an estimate from a rule seeing only the past; every statistic has a standard error [@harvey2015].
  2. takeaway ¶2 rolling_window is inclusive at both ends, so 60 gives 61 observations; almost no published result names its convention.
  3. takeaway ¶3 The loop stores a failed period as NA weights or a condition object and completes: right class, length and names.
  4. takeaway ¶4 The failures are not random: all within one year around the bear market's end; dropping them averages the easy months.
  5. takeaway ¶5 Always diagnose every period and carry the failure count into the result table; failures make a different object.
  6. takeaway ¶6 %dopar% without a registered backend runs on one core; eight workers cut 273 seconds to 61, results identical.
  7. takeaway ¶7 Under eq-25-se every interval overlaps every other; the differences in turnover, cost and feasibility are not estimates.

Note Seven against "around five"; nothing on the register's ranking (the claim the register's ¶3 says the book carries out of the table), on attribution or on look-ahead; bullets 3 to 5 split one finding in three.

26 The Cost of Forecast Error

7366 words · 5 sections · 36 paragraphs

Claim
For a mean-variance investor the cost of a wrong mean forecast is an identity, the squared Mahalanobis distance between forecast and truth (RAFE²/2γ), so the RMSE prices the error in the wrong metric; on the modeled market the RAFE tracks the unconstrained plug-in's Sharpe shortfall where the RMSE does not, which channel costs more depends on the unit, and in the register only the tangency portfolio loses more than a standard error to estimation.
Question
What does an error in a return forecast cost, and in which units should the cost be written; cites Salcher–Stöckl–Hanke in the second sentence for the answer (the optimizer's own risk metric) and Chopra–Ziemba in the third for the older number (means about ten times as costly as variances).
Why here
The text places the chapter on neither side of ch. 3's horizon axis, as measurement for strategic estimates and tactical forecasts alike. It takes the objective from ch. 3, the data from ch. 2, the excess basis and θ from ch. 9, Merton's precision result and the volatility clustering from ch. 4, the three forecasts and the market proxy from ch. 6, eq-08-kz, the inflation, the helper programs, the channel table and the stand-in truth from ch. 8, the term-spread forecast and its tilt from ch. 16, and the register and its standard error from ch. 25. It hands the training of a forecaster on the RAFE to ch. 27 and model error to ch. 28. Why it opens the part is not stated.
Does
Compute the RAFE, the certainty-equivalent loss and the Sharpe shortfall of a forecast or position; score forecasts on rolling train and test windows against the RMSE; split a plug-in rule's expected loss into mean noise, inversion error and amplification and check it by simulation; bound the Sharpe ratio a forecast's R² allows; compute a strategy's perfect-foresight shortfall.
Order
Opening (question, placement and roadmap, data), theory (identity and RAFE, Sharpe bound, RMSE as special case, covariance channel, information bound), implementation (functions and worked box, three forecasts on rolling windows, the covariance channel by simulation, the bound on the term-spread forecast), application (channel shares at three windows, shortfall by episode, the register), takeaways, exercises. Deviations: no main heading has a paragraph of its own; the implementation is 45 percent of the chapter and reads the main results, so the application adds three further cuts and its first subsection re-reads the simulation; the application opens on a table chunk and has no figure; the one figure sits in the implementation and no sentence refers to it; three quantities defined in the theory (C-RAFE, the intermediate nested measures, the multivariate bound) are never computed in the body; the packages block is the hidden setup chunk; six takeaways, four exercises.

The Loss in Risk Units 1585 words

The certainty equivalent and the plug-in loss 393 words

Says
Whitening positions and means by the square root of Σ turns the loss of any position into a squared distance, and for a plug-in position with Σ known that distance is the RAFE, the Mahalanobis distance between forecast and truth.
Why here
Roadmap: the identity comes first and the rest is read off it; needs ch. 3's objective and the θ of eq-09-tangency.
  1. definition ¶1 CE, optimum and CE* = θ²/2γ from ch. 3's objective (eq-26-ce); positions and means in risk units (eq-26-whiten).
  2. derivation ¶2 a lists the Sharpe ratios of N orthogonal directions; any position's loss is a squared distance, eq-26-loss-identity.
  3. derivation ¶3 Klein–Bawa and Kan–Zhou's loss; Σ known gives eq-26-rafe; RAFE defined; three readings; error maximization; the sample mean costs N/T.

Note ¶3 is 250 words around one display and does five jobs (attribution, the plug-in step, the definition of the RAFE, its three readings with the Michaud and Best–Grauer mechanism, the N/T price tied to eq-08-kz and Merton); the chapter's central term is defined in a "where" clause after its equation.

From the certainty equivalent to the Sharpe ratio 197 words

Says
A Sharpe-ratio investor sees only the angle between forecast and truth in risk units, so the shortfall is θ(1 − cos φ) and is bounded by c₀ times the RAFE.
Why here
First sentence: direction without length turns the identity into a bound; needs eq-26-rafe.
  1. derivation ¶1 eq-26-cos: the shortfall depends on the angle only; geometry gives RAFE ≥ θ sin φ, hence eq-26-bound; the squared-Sharpe version.

Note The subsection is one sentence chain around two displays; the reason for the case c₀ = 1 follows the display that states it; the squared-Sharpe decomposition arrives in the last sentence and is not used again.

The RMSE as a special case 283 words

Says
The RMSE is the last of a nested sequence of simplifications of Σ, each discarding something the optimizer uses, and the cited correlations with the shortfall fall along the sequence.
Why here
First sentence: the usual score replaces Σ in eq-26-rafe by something simpler; closes on ch. 27, which draws the consequence for training.
  1. definition ¶1 Salcher–Stöckl–Hanke's nested sequence, eq-26-nested: full Σ, constant correlation, zero correlation, one volatility, identity.
  2. evidence ¶2 What each step discards; their error–shortfall correlations fall from RAFE to RMSE; Kandel–Stambaugh, Cenesizoglu–Timmermann; ch. 27 draws the consequence.

Note ¶2 joins the reading of the sequence, the cited numbers, two further sources and the forward pointer in 199 words; the three intermediate measures are defined here and computed only in exercise 2.

The covariance channel 419 words

Says
With Σ estimated a second error enters, bounded path by path by θ times the C-RAFE, and in expectation the loss splits into mean noise, inversion error and noise amplified by the inverse.
Why here
First clause: Σ is now estimated too, which lifts the assumption held since eq-26-rafe; it derives the eq-08-kz that ch. 8 only quoted.
  1. derivation ¶1 Ĉ and the triangle inequality give eq-26-crafe; Wishart moments (eq-26-wishart) give the three-term split eq-26-kz-split; terms read; three caveats.

Note One unbroken paragraph of 415 words through three displays: a pathwise bound, an expectation, the reading of three terms, and three caveats (ch. 8's substitution design misses the third term, long-only caps all three, heavy tails raise the constants); the C-RAFE is defined here and computed only in exercise 1.

An upper bound from the forecast's information 286 words

Says
A forecast with explanatory power R² allows at most a conditional squared Sharpe ratio of (S₀² + R²)/(1 − R²), with a multivariate version, tight only for the unconstrained conditional tangency position.
Why here
Stated in its first two sentences: the measures above price a forecast that exists, a bound prices one that could.
  1. derivation ¶1 One asset: Campbell–Thompson's ceiling eq-26-bound1; multivariate eq-26-boundn; tight for the unconstrained conditional tangency, slack for restrained uses; estimated with inflation.

Note One paragraph of 278 words through two displays; the multivariate bound is stated and never evaluated in the chapter; the closing caveat (the sample bound inherits eq-08-inflation) does not return where the bound is computed.

The two channels at 36, 60 and 120 months 323 words

Where the shortfall is largest 535 words

Says
Sorted by the episode a sixty-month window ends in, the unconstrained rule is worst after the credit crisis and the long-only rule after the rates shock, and equal RAFEs cost different shortfalls because the shortfall reads the angle.
Why here
Stated: the rolling test window cannot reach the last two episodes; against the full-sample truth every estimation window can be scored.

Key Takeaways 266 words

Says
The identity, the shortfall bound and the RMSE as special case with the pooled correlations, the long-only metric, the three-term covariance channel and its dependence on the unit, the R² ceiling on the term spread, and the register's shortfalls.
Why here
Contract position.
  1. takeaway ¶1 For a mean-variance investor the CE loss of a wrong mean is RAFE²/2γ, a squared Mahalanobis distance; an identity.
  2. takeaway ¶2 Shortfall is θ(1 − cos φ), bounded by c₀·RAFE; RMSE is RAFE with Σ = I; pooled correlations, unconstrained plug-in.
  3. takeaway ¶3 Long-only: the RAFE in the metric the position was built in explains the shortfall; realized-covariance RAFE trails RMSE twice.
  4. takeaway ¶4 Covariance channel in three terms; larger in CE units at 36 and 60 months, smaller in Sharpe units always (Chopra–Ziemba).
  5. takeaway ¶5 R² allows at most θ_c² = (S₀² + R²)/(1 − R²); the term spread's allowance and what the tilt took.
  6. takeaway ¶6 Register shortfalls follow the order of estimation at both ends; minimum CVaR the exception, its benchmark minimizing a tail.

Note Six bullets; ¶2 holds three statements; ¶4 counts the mean's noise N/T as a term of the covariance channel, where the theory calls it the mean channel and the application's table leaves it out of the covariance share; nothing on where the shortfall is largest.

27 Decision-Aware Forecasting

7998 words · 5 sections · 44 paragraphs

Claim
A mean-variance investor pays for forecast error in the metric Σ⁻¹ and not in squared error, so a forecaster trained on mean squared error is trained on the wrong loss; of three corrections, shrinking the mean in risk units adds the most Sharpe ratio on the modeled market, acting on fewer forecast dimensions adds nothing beyond what the forecast was already worth, and tuning on the certainty equivalent gives a worse forecast with a better portfolio inside sampling error, while none of the three beats the rules that use no mean.
Question
Should a return forecaster be trained on mean squared error at all; cites Stöckl and Hanke in the second sentence for the closed-form answer (the certainty equivalent lost is the error in risk units, correctable after the fact) and Elmachtoub and Grigas for the general operations-research form (train on the regret of the decision).
Why here
The text says the chapter sits on neither side of chapter 3's horizon axis: it is about estimation, and the corrections apply to a long-run and to a conditional mean alike. It takes the risk-adjusted forecast error and its Sharpe-gap bound from chapter 26 as given, the investor and the certainty equivalent from chapter 3, Bayes-Stein from chapter 6, the condition number from chapter 7, the Best–Grauer mechanism, the N/T inflation, the plug-in rule and the covariance channel from chapter 8, the minimum-variance formula from chapter 9, the term-spread forecast, the tilt and the elastic net from chapter 16, the parametric policy from chapter 18, per-class costs from chapter 23 and the register from chapter 25. It hands to chapter 28 the case in which the model behind the loss is itself in doubt. That it follows chapter 26 is implied by that borrowing; why it sits in this part is not stated.
Does
Price a forecast error in the investor's units; correct a sample mean toward a target with a constant that needs no tuning; act on a forecast at a chosen granularity, with the number of groups read from an information coefficient; tune a forecaster's penalty on the certainty equivalent of the tilt it implies; score all of it on the register's months.
Order
Opening (question, placement and roadmap, data), theory (the two losses, then one subsection per answer in the order after the forecast, between forecast and decision, inside the training loop), implementation (the same three answers in the same order, the first over two subsections), application (one scoreboard figure and three paragraphs), takeaways, exercises. Deviations: neither the theory nor the implementation heading has a paragraph of its own; all four tables are read in the implementation, including the one the text calls the result the chapter was built to show, so the application computes nothing new and re-reads those numbers from one figure; the theory ends on the failure conditions of all three answers, placed under the third answer's heading; the packages block is the hidden setup chunk and the first visible chunk loads data; no callout and no PortfolioAnalytics call anywhere; five takeaways, four exercises.

The statistical loss and the decision loss 515 words

Says
Squared error and the certainty-equivalent loss are quadratic forms in the same error under the identity and under Σ⁻¹; they rank forecasts differently, by up to the condition number, and a forecaster trained on the first can be reconciled with the second in three places.
Why here
Needs chapter 3's investor and chapter 26's risk-adjusted error, which the text says it takes from there; its last sentence names the three places that the next three subsections take up in that order.
  1. derivation ¶1 eq-27-mse, the squared error; chapter 3's investor holds the plug-in position; the certainty-equivalent loss is a perfect square, eq-27-decision.
  2. definition ¶2 The quadratic form is the squared risk-adjusted forecast error [Salcher et al.], taken from chapter 26; it is Elmachtoub–Grigas's regret.
  3. derivation ¶3 Two metrics, one error: per eigen-direction the decision loss divides by the eigenvalue; the ratio is chapter 7's condition number.
  4. code metric cost of a unit error along the first and the last eigenvector, and the log ratio
  5. result ¶4 Unit costs and ratio; squared error buys accuracy where variance is large [Best–Grauer]; lineage [Granger; Elliott–Timmermann]; three places to reconcile.

Note ¶4 does four jobs: it reads the number, gives the mechanism, gives the lineage and announces the three answers, which is the roadmap for the rest of the chapter; RAFE appears in eq-27-decision one paragraph before it is named.

Correcting the mean in risk units 768 words

Says
In risk units the sample mean's expected squared error is N/T; shrinking its deviation from a pre-set target by a James-Stein factor has an exact risk, minimized by a constant that depends on N, T and the target's rank alone, and for the grand mean the corrected mean dominates the sample mean whenever T exceeds N.
Why here
Stated at the end of the previous subsection: the first of the three places, after the forecast, with the model left alone. The implementation codes eq-27-corrector and eq-27-constant directly, and the next subsection sets its hard reduction against this soft one.
  1. derivation ¶1 Kan–Zhou's setting; whitened, the sample mean is normal with covariance I/T; the expected squared RAFE is N/T, eq-27-price.
  2. claim ¶2 Each dimension costs 1/T whatever signal it carries; this N/T inflates chapter 8's promised Sharpe ratio.
  3. definition ¶3 Stein estimation in risk units toward a fixed target, zero or the grand mean; the square root cancels; eq-27-corrector [Stöckl–Hanke].
  4. derivation ¶4 Grand-mean reading; Wishart ratio and Stein's identity give the exact risk, eq-27-risk; best constant eq-27-constant; dominance for T above N.
  5. evidence ¶5 Lineage: Stein, James–Stein, Efron–Morris, Jorion; new is a non-orthogonal target; Bayes-Stein not covered; zero target rescales only; no-mean rules unranked.

Note ¶4 runs through two numbered displays and holds the derivation, the optimal constant and the dominance result in one paragraph; ¶5 is two paragraphs in one, the lineage and then three limits of the theorem, and those limits are what the implementation later runs into.

Using less of the forecast 431 words

Says
Acting on a k-dimensional projection of the forecast loses the signal discarded plus k/T of noise retained, so a dimension is worth acting on only if its squared Sharpe ratio exceeds 1/T; the familiar rules are fixed cells of a granularity grid, and the number of rank groups worth forming follows from the information coefficient by a cube-root rule.
Why here
The second of the three places, between forecast and decision; the text says it starts from the same identity and sets hard reduction against the shrinkage of the previous subsection. eq-27-hard and eq-27-groups are what the granularity experiment implements.
  1. derivation ¶1 Keep the forecast, act on a k-dimensional projection [Salcher et al.]; loss is signal discarded plus k/T noise retained, eq-27-hard.
  2. claim ¶2 A 1/T threshold per dimension; shrinkage is soft reduction; plug-in, minimum variance, 1/N, sorts and tilts are fixed grid cells.
  3. derivation ¶3 Information coefficient ρ [Grinold], profile linear in rank: the best number of groups is a cube root, eq-27-groups.
  4. claim ¶4 Cattaneo et al.'s rate, but each group costs 1/T however many assets it averages; G* is small, down to 1/N.

Note The truth in risk units is introduced a second time under new symbols (a, b̂, η), one subsection after m and m̂; ¶2 holds three points (the threshold, soft against hard, the grid of rules) and eight citations; the grid is given in one sentence, with no table or figure.

The decision loss inside the training loop 437 words

Says
The decision-aware estimator maximizes the sample certainty equivalent of the portfolio the forecast implies; with the plug-in map and a known Σ it is least squares reweighted by Σ⁻¹, and without the closed form it still applies to any utility, map and model; what it loses is precision, and each of the three answers has a condition under which it does not help.
Why here
The third of the three places, inside the training loop; the text ties it back to chapter 18's policy as the special case and to the first answer through eq-27-gls. Its closing paragraph sets up the implementation by saying where each answer should fail.
  1. definition ¶1 eq-27-loop: maximize the sample certainty equivalent of the implied portfolio; Brandt's policy the special case; GLS equivalence eq-27-gls; generalizes.
  2. caveat ¶2 A realized certainty equivalent is as noisy as a Sharpe ratio [Lo]; the condition under which each answer fails.

Note ¶1 runs through two displays and does four things (defines the estimator, names the lineage, derives the equivalence, claims generality); ¶2 closes the whole theory section with the failure conditions of all three answers under the third answer's heading, and ends on a forward pointer that one answer will partly fail without saying which.
